_He goes back to the house, and the door shuts quietly. It is the first right thing you have seen him do._  
_You come out of the shed, and open the lantern, and hold it low again. She works on until nearly dawn._  

### Step 11: Walk the little grey runner home to the Grove.

Hint: Go slowly. It has never walked further than the end of the straight. · check: `escort:s27-nine:grove`

#### Scene `s27-letgo`

_By breakfast half the valley is at the Circuit fence. Nobody arranged it; people just came._  
_Along the whole finishing straight, in letters as tall as a Nubbin, the frost says: HOME._  
**Fern** _(happy)_: HOME. Look at the size of it. She must have run out of rail.  
_Mr Tull is at the gate with his cart. He reads the rail, and then the faces along the fence._  
**Mr Tull** _(think)_: Morning, Mr Vale. I’ve come for whatever didn’t place. But I’ll not take that one.  
**Mr Tull**: Its name’s been up on your rails all summer. I’d have half this valley at my gate by Sunday, asking after it.  
**Mr Tull**: I sell runners. I don’t sell the ones people ask after. Good day to you.  
_He tips his hat to the fence, quite politely, and drives away over the pass. His cart is lighter by one._  
_Corvin stands at the end of the straight with his hands in his pockets. He reads the rail one more time._  
_Hob is at the pen behind the house. He opens his mouth to say “Nine”, and stops, and says its name instead._  
_He says it carefully, like a word in another language. Corvin pretends not to hear._  
**Corvin Vale**: Take it. It’s no use to me famous.  
_He says it to the rail, not to you. Then he goes back to the house without looking round._  
_The little grey runner looks at the open pen for a long time. Then it follows you to the road, three steps behind._  

#### Done `s27-gate`

_At the Grove gate you lift and push. The little grey runner stops on the threshold._  
_It looks at the gate as if a gate is a thing that might shut on it._  
_Your Nubbin hops out of your pocket, goes through, and sits on the far side, waiting._  
_After a long while, the runner comes through too. It sits down by the gatepost and looks at everything._  

### Step 12: Get Spindle’s egg sac down from the shed rafters, and take it somewhere warm for the winter.

Hint: It’s too high to reach from the crate. Somebody small and careful will have to climb. · check: `carry:s27-egg-sac:grove`

#### Scene `s27-egg-sac`

_Autumn comes on properly. Up at the Circuit the shed window has fogged at the corners, and the swallows have gone._  
_Spindle is slower now. She sits in the sun on the sill more than she sits in her web._  
_High in the rafters, where the roof meets the wall, there is something new: a little round sac of silk, the colour of straw._  
_It is the best thing she has ever made. You can see that from the floor._  
**Sparks** _(soft)_: Her eggs. Hundreds, likely. They sleep in that all winter and come out in the spring.  
**Sparks**: But it’ll freeze up there. Everything up here freezes, come winter. And she can’t carry it. Not now.  
_In the web, very small, a new word: PLEASE._  

#### Done `s27-carry`

_Sparks carries her long ladder in from the yard, sideways, swearing gently at the door frame._  
**Sparks**: Rafters are your job. I’ve got the knees of an eighty-year-old and the opinions to match.  
_Your Nubbin looks at the ladder, and at the sac, and at you. Then it climbs, one rung at a time, with enormous care._  
_It comes down backwards with the sac held against its chest, the way you’d carry an egg that might still hatch today._  
_Spindle watches every rung, and doesn’t breathe out until it is on the floor. You don’t either._  
**Sparks**: Grove lantern shed. Top shelf, over the warm pipe. Nothing up there ever freezes, not even my tea.  
_You carry it home down the valley in your two cupped hands. Spindle stays in her window; she has done her part._  

Sets `{"s27-carrier":"nubbin"}`

### Step 13: Sit with Spindle in the shed window.

Hint: Take the crate by the window. There’s nothing to do. That’s the point. · check: `sit:s27-last-evening`

#### Scene `s27-last-evening`

_The next evening you go up to the shed with nothing to fetch and nowhere to be._  
_Spindle is on the sill in the last of the sun, with her legs drawn in. She lifts one, to say hello, and puts it down again._  
_You sit on the crate. The light goes orange, and then gold, and then the colour of the inside of a shell._  
_You want to say something. You think of everything she did with words, and how few you have._  
_Hazel would know what to say. You think Hazel would also say: say the true thing, and say it plainly._  
_So you do._  
**You** _(soft)_: You have been my friend.  
_Spindle is quiet for a long time. Then she climbs, very slowly, to the corner of her web._  
_She writes one more word. It is very small, the smallest she has ever written, and it is just for you._  
_WELL._  

#### Done `s27-walk-home`

_You walk home in the dark with your Nubbin asleep in your pocket._  
_You don’t look back at the window. You think she would prefer it that way; she was always very polite._  

### Step 14: Tell Sparks.

Hint: She’s at the start lamp. She already knows. Tell her anyway. · check: `talk:sparks:s27-empty-web`

#### Scene `s27-empty-web`

_In the morning the frost is on the rails as usual. You go to the shed first._  
_The web in the window is still there. The sun comes through it and lays a pattern on the bench._  
_It is empty. The last word is still in the corner, very small: WELL._  
_Your Nubbin climbs out of your pocket onto the bench, and sits under the web, and looks up at it._  
_It doesn’t do anything. It just sits next to it, the way you sat next to her._  
_You stand in the doorway for a while. Then you go to find Sparks._  

#### Done `s27-leave-it`

_Sparks doesn’t say anything for a long time, which for Sparks is a very long time._  
**Sparks** _(sad)_: Leave it.  
**Sparks** _(sad)_: The web. Leave it up. Nobody sweeps that window. Nobody touches it. Not ever.  
_Nobody does. It stays in the corner of the shed window, empty, catching the light, and Corvin never has it swept._  

#### Complete `s27-complete`

_A week later Sparks comes down the valley with something in her fist. She opens it on your kitchen table._  
_It is a drawer label off her old cabinet, in her square capitals. SPARE._  
**Sparks** _(soft)_: She learned her S’s off that one. Every S she ever wrote. Somebody’s. That one.  
_Fern has sewn SOMEBODY’S onto a strip of ribbon, a bit crooked, and given one to anybody who stood at the fence._  
_Pixel has made a pane for the Grove lantern shed window. On frosty mornings, a name comes up on it in white._  
_Up at the Circuit, Hob has started calling the runners by names. He makes them up, and some of them are terrible._  
_Corvin pretends not to notice. Once, passing the pens, he is heard to say “morning, Gravel” to the third from the end._  

**Rewards**

- The Frost-Web Window (decor): A pane for the Grove lantern shed. On winter mornings the frost picks out a web on it, and in the web, in white, the name Spindle wrote.
- SOMEBODY’S Ribbon (cosmetic): A strip of ribbon with SOMEBODY’S sewn on it, a bit crooked, by Fern. For any Nubbin who is.
- Spindle’s Drawer Label: “SPARE” (item): A keepsake for the journal. The label off Sparks’s cabinet drawer that Spindle learned her S’s from.
- Spindle’s three (feature): From the first spring, three tiny spiders live in the Grove lantern shed. They write very small words now and then. Ambient only: no care, no chores.
- boughtBack (flag): Sold path only. Your sold Nubbin comes home, and the Collector ending can now come only from keeping the stone.

Sets on completion: `{"s27Done":true}`

**Learning Grove swaps**

- `s27-last-evening#6` → I’m so glad it was you in the window, Spindle.
- `s27-spring#9` → Not many friends can spell, and not many spellers stay up all night for a friend.
- `s27-spring-wisp#9` → Not many friends can spell, and not many spellers stay up all night for a friend.

**Notes:** THEME. A small life saved by words, and a friend who writes them and then dies, as everything does, in her season. The gentlest answer to Corvin, who keeps Nubbins quiet and numbered: a spider gives one a name the whole valley can read, written in the very frost that stills his runners. Corvin is the man the valley embarrassed into doing right. SIGNATURE CHOICE (s27-was-it-you, step 6 done; s27-truth): shield (“Yes, it was me”), frost (“Ask the frost”: the truth, slantwise) or silent. PAYOFF four steps later in step 10’s done scene: frost or silent plays s27-midnight (Corvin finds Spindle mid-letter at midnight, lifts his cold hand over her, lowers it: “Spell it right, then.” His first right act in the story); shield plays s27-midnight-shield (VARIANTS: Corvin watches YOU all night from his window and never once looks at the rail; he never learns about Spindle). Both are fine; neither changes the outcome. OUTLINE MAPPING. 18 outline quests in 14 steps: q1→1, q2→2, q3→3, q4→4, q5→5, q6+q7→6, q8→7 and 8 (split: the word, then the move), q9→8 done, q10+q11→9, q12 and q13’s night→10, q13’s morning+q14→11, q15→12, q16→13, q17→14, q18→the spring VARIANTS (post-game). TIMING. A step’s scene plays the moment the previous step’s check is met; its done scene plays when its own check is met. Exceptions: s27-wire plays on the first visit to the Circuit (Races, or the road up the valley) after c5-close and c5-sold; s27-wait plays on the NEXT Circuit visit after s27-tam (a real day between); s27-first-night and s27-second-night play at the next dusk at the Circuit; done scenes marked dawn play at the next first light the player is at the Circuit (no fail state, no timer: the frost is there every dawn until it is seen). Steps 1–8 belong to chapters 5–6 (high summer to summer’s end; frost on the rails in August). s27-ready (step 9) waits until c7-open has played (the first autumn dawn). s27-egg-sac (step 12) waits until wisp is set (S2 settled) OR chapter 8 has opened (S2 never played, or closed unfinished). CLOSING. The line must close before chapter 9 opens. If c8-close has played and step 11 is not done, the story closes unfinished: no boughtBack, no Nine, no rewards; chapter 9 runs as before. If step 11 is done but steps 12–14 are not, they close unfinished too (no s27Done, no rewards, no spring scene), but boughtBack and the Nubbin that came home stay. PATHS. Sold path = soldNubbin && !boughtBack when S27 opens (in practice soldNubbin). The runner at the end of the row is painted “9” on both paths, so most scenes are shared: on the sold path it is your sold Nubbin (soldId; c5-sold already showed its painted number), on the runner path a new runner Hob calls Nine. Sold-path replacements (VARIANTS): s27-row-sold for s27-row; s27-words-sold for s27-words (no naming choice: it already has the name you gave it); s27-home for s27-letgo (THE CAMPAIGN HOOK, below); s27-gate-sold for s27-gate. CARRIER (step 12 done). wisp = spared or sable → s27-carry-wisp (sets s27-carrier = wisp; Wisp has trailed you up the valley after the sun-warm sac; he keeps it warm under the cottage step, where he lives, until spring). Otherwise, if s21Done → s27-carry-clank; else s27-carry (both set s27-carrier = nubbin: your current Nubbin climbs Sparks’s ladder; the sac winters on the warmest shelf of the Grove lantern shed). SPRING (post-game). In the first spring after the ending, the first morning the player is in the Grove, play s27-spring (s27-carrier = nubbin) or s27-spring-wisp (s27-carrier = wisp). Only if s27Done. It is ambient and ending-neutral; queue it after any epilogue and after S24 if both are due that morning. Then spawn Spindle’s three (see REWARDS). CAMPAIGN HOOK: s27-home (Tier 1). Plays INSTEAD of s27-letgo, as step 11’s scene, on the sold path. Corvin names his price, which is what he paid you: 200 Shells. Choices: pay (set boughtBack: true, s27-paid: shells) or hat (the Keepers pass a hat; Fern puts in a button; set boughtBack: true, s27-paid: hat). Readers of boughtBack, unchanged: endingFor (the Collector ending can now come only from keptStone); c9-partner, c9-open-sold and c9-shard-collector (they no longer play: chapter 9 opens with c9-open and the shard scene follows the corvin flag); end-collector-sold (can no longer play; end-collector-kept still can). c5-sold has already played. A later c9-open bargain sets boughtBack again, which is harmless: its 200 Shells are Ash’s price. SIM: (1) In applyChoice, add a side effect for scene s27-home, choice pay or hat, only if soldNubbin && !boughtBack before the choice’s set is applied (guard: snapshot the flag first, since choice.set writes boughtBack). pay: shells = max(0, shells − 200), as c9-open’s bargain does; hat: no Shells change. Then bring the soldId stray home exactly as c9-open’s bargain does: find it in world.strays by soldId; if hasRoom(world), remove it from strays, put it beside the keeper, trust = 20, stray = false, behavior.state = sit, push it to world.nubbins. (2) IF THE GROVE IS FULL: leave it in strays but set its nubbin.trust = 20 and its returnsAtMs = world.timeMs, so the existing stray-return loop in sim/trust.ts brings it to the gate as soon as there is room (it arrives as a stray; that loop’s Math.max(n.trust, 8) keeps the 20). s27-gate-sold is written to work either way. (3) If soldNubbin is true but no soldId stray exists (it was lost some other way), still set boughtBack (the story happened) and take the Shells; skip the homecoming. (4) It stays grey (shadow = true); the painted 9 is cosmetic and fades by the first spring if the renderer can show it. (5) RUNNER PATH: when s27-letgo ends, create a new Nubbin for Nine: adult, grey (shadow = true), run-leaning stats, trust 20, named from flag s27-nine-name; push it to world.strays with returnsAtMs = world.timeMs so it arrives at the gate as a stray you can recruit (feed or pet to settle it). If s27-nine-name is the empty string (choice own), open the existing naming box at that choice and write the typed name into s27-nine-name; if left blank, use Tansy. (6) Render the name on the rail in s27-first-word from s27-nine-name (runner) or the soldId Nubbin’s name (sold); the dialogue text never prints it, so no placeholder is needed. (7) The Frost-Web Window decor shows the same name. (8) Spindle’s three: three tiny ambient spider sprites in the Grove lantern shed from the spring scene on; now and then a tiny web word (EVENING, WAIT, WELL, WARM, HOME) appears in the shed window at dawn. No needs, no care, no chores. (9) Tier 2: Spindle’s empty web stays in the Circuit shed window for the rest of the game; Hob uses names after the Show. ART. Web words are drawn as silk letters; at dawn they rime white. Spindle is a grey-brown garden spider no bigger than a fingernail, drawn kindly: round body, neat legs, no fangs. The little grey runner (Nine) is the smallest grey runner, with a white painted 9. Sound: the lantern’s hiss in s27-home-word; silence in s27-empty-web. CANON. Spindle never speaks: every web word is narrated. Nubbins and the runners never speak. Wisp whispers in his S2 lowercase half-voice (“mine. warm. mine.”). Clank speaks (S21; a robot). Hazel is away all through this story: she is only remembered, never present, and her whereabouts are never said. The third rule is echoed (WAIT; the crate by the window) but never quoted. Fern’s word recalls Bramble, whom she lost (seeded in chapter 1); S8 is not referenced. Corvin’s shard is never named: his cold hand, his breast pocket and the frost that doesn’t melt carry it. LEARNING GROVE (for the edu writer). Spiders have eight legs and make silk; some spin egg sacs, and spiderlings “balloon” away on threads in the wind. Frost forms when water in the air freezes on cold surfaces, which is why a web turns white. Reading and spelling with a spider: build each web word letter by letter (WAIT, WARM, HOME, WELL). Life cycles: autumn endings, spring beginnings. Spindle’s death stays offscreen in both bands.

## S28. Please Look After This Nubbin

*Paddington (Michael Bond’s books from 1958, and the films Paddington and Paddington 2): the polite stranger with a label round his neck, the grumpy neighbour, the marmalade and the hard stare (homage; two approved one-sentence quotes)* · audience: all · where: The Grove gate (the carrier’s cart); Byte’s house, with Mr Grue’s house and glasshouse next door; the market square; the cart stop on the valley road; the bench by Hazel’s cottage door · when: Opens the morning after chapter 8’s notebook (c8-close). Steps 1–10 run from early autumn into early winter, all before chapter 9 opens. Steps 11–13 run in chapter 11, after c11-open. Closes before chapter 12.

**Cast:** Parcel (Paddington Bear (Michael Bond), homage only: a small brown Nubbin, never the bear), Mr Grue (Mr Curry, the Browns’ grumpy next-door neighbour (Michael Bond’s Paddington books), reference only), The carter, Dot Amblin, Granny Brock

### Step 1: Take the stray from the carrier’s cart to Byte’s house.

Hint: Walk slowly and it will follow you. Byte’s house is the one with the very straight hedge, next to the glasshouse. · check: `escort:s28-parcel:byte-house`

#### Scene `s28-cart`

_The morning after the Hollow. You slept badly, with Hazel’s notebook under your pillow._  
_Hooves in the lane. The carrier’s cart pulls up at the gate, and the carter leans down without getting off._  
**The carter**: Morning. Got a passenger for this end of the valley. Wouldn’t give its name.  
_On a crate in the back sits a small brown Nubbin, very upright, with a basket on its knees._  
_It sees you, and bows. Not a nod: a proper bow, from the middle._  
_Round its neck, on a piece of string, hangs a luggage tag._  
_“Please look after this one. Thank you.”_  
_Underneath, smaller: “For Byte. H.”_  
**The carter**: Never saw who put it on. It was sat on the milestone at the ford, with a plum on its head for the fare.  
**The carter** _(happy)_: Plum was very fair. I’ve had worse off people.  
_You know that handwriting. You have read a letter in it so many times the folds have gone soft._  
_The ink has run a little, in some old rain. It doesn’t say where she is, or how she is._  
_The Nubbin climbs down off the cart with great care, one step at a time, and waits by your boot._  

#### Done `s28-for-byte`

_Byte opens his door before you knock. He has a pencil behind each ear, in case one runs out._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: That’s a Nubbin. Why have you brought me a Nubbin? I didn’t order a Nubbin.  
_The Nubbin bows to him, from the middle. After a long moment Byte bows back, very slightly, in case it’s a trick._  
_He reads the tag, then the back of the tag, which is blank, then the front again._  
**Byte** _(think)_: “For Byte. H.” Who’s H.?  

**Choices**

- **Tell him it’s Hazel’s hand.** — sets `{"s28-told":true}`
    - **You**: It’s Hazel’s writing. I’d know it anywhere.
    - Byte goes very still. He holds the tag up to the window, as if her hand might show through the card.
    - **Byte**: She’s been gone since the snow. Where did it get on?
    - **You**: The ford. That’s all the carter knows.
    - **Byte**: Then that’s all we know. Right. I’ll want to work out the rest.
- **Keep it to yourself for now.** — sets `{"s28-told":false}`
    - You let the question go by. It’s only an initial; there must be dozens of H’s in the valley.
    - **Byte**: Unknown sender. I’ll make a list of everyone with an H. It’ll be a short list.
    - It won’t be. He will be very thorough about it.

### Step 2: Fetch Hazel’s marmalade from her pantry for the stray.

Hint: The jars are on the top shelf of the pantry in Hazel’s cottage. Carry them to Byte’s house. · check: `carry:s28-jars:byte-house`

#### Scene `s28-one-jar`

_In the Nubbin’s basket there is a scarf, a dried apple ring and an empty jar, licked very clean._  
_The jar has a label, in the same leaning hand: “Marmalade. Hollow. Last of.”_  
**Byte** _(think)_: It’s lived on this. All the way down the valley. On one jar.  
**Byte**: Nubbins eat fruit. Fruit. It’s in every book. Nobody writes “marmalade” in a book.  
_The Nubbin holds the jar out to him upside down, to show him it’s empty. Then it waits, politely, in case he has another._  
_You remember a shelf in Hazel’s pantry. Jars with paper lids and string, all in a row, all the same orange._  
**You**: I think I know where there’s more.  
**Byte**: How many?  
**You**: Some.  
**Byte** _(think)_: “Some” isn’t a number. Go and get “some”. I’ll make a note.  

#### Done `s28-received`

_Six jars, with paper lids and string. Under the sixth, a recipe card in Hazel’s hand, spotted with old orange._  
_Byte files the card under R, then counts the jars. Then he counts them again, in case one moved._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Six. Right. And you’re… one. Received.  
**Byte** _(soft)_: Parcel. That’s your name. Parcel, received. I’ll sign for you.  
_He writes PARCEL in his notebook and underlines it. Parcel bows._  
_Your own Nubbin has its face in the open jar. When it comes out it looks alarmed, and goes straight back in._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: That’s one spoon unaccounted for already.  

### Step 3: Help Byte with Parcel’s bath, and then with the water.

Hint: Mop from the bathroom outwards, towards the front door. The water is heading for the garden fence. · check: `minigame:clear:s28-mop-up`

#### Scene `s28-bath`

_Parcel came down from the Hollow on a cart, and a good deal of the Hollow came with it._  
**Byte** _(think)_: It’s muddy to the knees. Has it got knees? It’s muddy to where knees would be.  
**Byte**: Bath. Standard procedure. I’ll run it. You find a towel.  
_Byte puts the plug in, shows Parcel the taps, and goes to look for the bath thermometer._  
_Parcel, wishing to be helpful, turns both taps on full._  
_Then it sits on the edge of the bath, very upright, and waits to be told when to stop._  
_Nobody tells it._  
_By the time you find a towel, the bath is full. By the time Byte finds the thermometer, so is the landing._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: WHY IS THE HALL WET?  
_The water goes down the stairs, along the hall, under the front door and out into the garden. It keeps going._  
_Next door is a very tidy garden, and water likes a tidy garden. It goes under the fence to see._  

#### Done `s28-nubbin`

_A head comes up over the fence, then the rest of a man: a cardigan, a moustache and an expression._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: NUBBIN!  
_Mr Grue, from next door. He doesn’t say anything else, and he doesn’t need to._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Send it back where it came from!  
**Byte** _(think)_: It stays until I’ve verified the sender. That’s procedure.  
_Upstairs, Parcel has turned the taps off by itself. It appears at the bathroom window, clean and pleased, and bows to Mr Grue._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Don’t you bow at me.  

### Step 4: Be at Byte’s when Mr Grue comes round about his garden.

Hint: Go to Byte’s house in the morning. You won’t have to do anything. Probably. · check: `watch:s28-hard-stare`

#### Scene `s28-compensation`

_Next morning Mr Grue is on Byte’s step, with a sheet of paper, a pencil and his best cardigan._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: I’ve made a list.  
**Byte** _(think)_: So have I. Mine’s longer. Go on.  
**Mr Grue**: Item. Lettuces, drowned. Item. Path, unlevelled. Item. One pair of slippers, left on the step to air, now soup.  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Item. My peace of mind. I’ll want something for that. I’ll be generous. I’ll take it in coal.  
_Parcel comes out from behind Byte’s legs. It stands on the step, very upright, and looks at Mr Grue._  
_It doesn’t blink or move. It just looks, long and level, the way a lighthouse looks at the sea._  
_Byte, without taking his eyes off it, starts counting under his breath._  

#### Done `s28-eleven-seconds`

_Mr Grue stops. Mr Grue starts again, and stops._  
**Mr Grue** _(shock)_: Well. I…  
_He folds the list and puts it in his cardigan pocket. He goes home, and shuts his gate very quietly._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: Eleven seconds.  
**Byte**: Eleven. I’ve never seen anybody hold one past four. Not even Ember.  
_That evening Byte opens a fresh notebook and writes on the cover, in neat capitals: PARCEL: INCIDENTS._  
_Inside, he rules a column, INCIDENT, and a second, TIME. Then he looks at you over the top of it._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Any other columns? I don’t usually ask. I’m asking.  

**Choices**

- **Suggest a column called “Good things”.** — sets `{"s28-column":"good"}`
    - **Byte**: “Good things.” That isn’t a measurement.
    - He rules it anyway, at the end, narrower than the others. You notice he leaves room to widen it.
- **Suggest a column called “Unverified”.** — sets `{"s28-column":"unverified"}`
    - **Byte**: Yes. Good. For anything I can’t prove yet.
    - He rules it wide, and letters it in his best capitals. It is, you suspect, his favourite word.
- **Leave it. It’s his log.** — sets `{"s28-column":"none"}`
    - **You**: It’s your log, Byte.
    - **Byte**: …Yes. It is. Right.
    - He looks at his two columns for a while, as if one were missing and he couldn’t think which.

### Step 5: Follow Parcel when it sets off at eleven with half a sandwich.

Hint: Walk behind Parcel, not beside it: it likes to lead. It goes towards the foot of the hill. · check: `walk:s28-parcel:low-wall`

#### Scene `s28-sandwich`

_A week on, Parcel has a routine. Byte has written it down, with times._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Up at seven. Bows to the kettle. Marmalade. Tidies its basket. Bows to the kettle again.  
**Byte**: And at eleven it makes a sandwich, cuts it in half, and goes out with one half. Comes back without it.  
**Byte** _(think)_: I’ve followed it twice. It loses me at the wall. I think it does it on purpose.  
_The clock in the hall says five to eleven. In the kitchen, a knife goes down._  
_Parcel comes out with half a marmalade sandwich held in front of it in both paws, like a tray. It bows to you on the way past._  
**Byte**: Go on. You’re less noticeable than me. Everyone’s less noticeable than me.  

#### Done `s28-elevenses`

_The low wall at the foot of the hill, in the thin autumn sun. Moss is sitting on it, and these days you can see a little of the wall through him._  
_Parcel climbs up beside him, sets the half sandwich on the stone between them, very precisely, and bows._  
**Moss** _(soft)_: Elevenses.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: It brings one every morning. I don’t eat. It knows that. It brings one anyway. Somebody raised it properly.  
**Moss** _(happy)_: The robins have them, after. We sit, and I tell it things. The winter the pond froze with a duck in it. It bows at the end.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: Plenty have come down that road with a label and a small case, you know. Most of the good ones did.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: People think precious things shine. Mostly they’re a bit worn. They’ve been held a lot.  
_Footsteps. Byte comes round the end of the wall at four minutes past eleven, out of breath._  
**Moss**: His lace.  
_You look at Byte. You don’t say anything; you don’t have to._  
**Byte** _(think)_: It’s untied, isn’t it. He’s said it. You’ve got the face.  
_Byte ties it. Moss watches him tie it with great interest._  
**Moss**: Other one.  

### Step 6: See how Parcel gets on with Null.

Hint: Null is at Byte’s house. Sit back and watch: Parcel will do the introducing. · check: `watch:s28-parcel:null`

#### Scene `s28-null`

_Byte’s garden, late afternoon. Null comes out of the hedge after a moth with a leaf on its head, as usual._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Null, this is Parcel. Parcel, Null. Null chases things. Parcel bows at things. You’ll have a lot to talk about.  
_Parcel bows. Null has never been bowed at: it thinks about it, and falls over backwards, which is nearly the same._  
_Then Null sees the sandwich: cut into triangles, on a saucer, carried out by Parcel to share._  
_Null doesn’t want the bread. It takes only the marmalade, off the top, in one long lick, and runs._  
_Parcel looks at the bare bread, then at Null, long and level. Null looks straight back, chewing._  
_Nobody has ever held the stare before, and Parcel seems delighted. It gives Null the rest of the bread._  
**Byte** _(happy)_: They’ve adopted each other. I couldn’t tell you which one did it first. I wasn’t quick enough.  

#### Done `s28-logged`

_That night Byte writes it up. He lets you watch over his shoulder, which is new._  
_He writes it under INCIDENT. Then he stops, and writes it again, smaller, under GOOD THINGS._  
**Byte** _(think)_: It can be both. I’ve checked. There’s nothing in the rules.  

### Step 7: Go to market day with Byte to buy Parcel a coat, and keep an eye on Parcel.

Hint: Follow the noise across the square. Parcel likes looking at the tops of things. · check: `find:s28-parcel:market`

#### Scene `s28-market`

_Market day. The square below the bridge is all awnings and shouting and the smell of hot chestnuts._  
**Byte** _(think)_: It shivers in the mornings. I’ve logged it. It needs a coat. Something with fastenings it can manage.  
**Byte**: I’ve put the stalls in order of efficiency. We’ll do them in that order.  
_You do the first stall in that order. When you turn round from the second, Parcel isn’t there._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: Where is it? It was there. I had it in my peripheral vision. I have very good peripheral vision.  
_Somewhere across the square there is a noise like a xylophone falling downstairs._  
_Your Nubbin is off towards it already, nose first. It knows that smell: oranges, boiled with sugar._  

#### Done `s28-jam-stall`

_A preserves stall. There was a pyramid of jars on it, very tall, very proud, with one orange-coloured jar on top._  
_There is now a slope of jars. Parcel is at the bottom, holding the top jar up in both paws, unbroken._  
_Nothing has broken. Everything has simply rolled, very far, in every direction._  
**Dot Amblin** _(angry)_: My PYRAMID. Three hours, that pyramid took!  
_A crowd has gathered, so Parcel bows to it. The crowd claps, and starts picking up jars, and paying for them._  
**Dot Amblin** _(shock)_: Well. That’s the bramble jelly gone. And the damson. Oh, go on, then, take the plum.  
_By noon the stall has sold out. Dot Amblin sits on her empty table with her cash tin, looking at Parcel as if it were weather._  
**Dot Amblin** _(happy)_: Keep that one, little one. Apricot. On the house. Come back next market, and bring your bow.  
_Byte buys a knitted coat with toggles, brown, Parcel-sized. Then a second, smaller, for your Nubbin._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Parity. If one of them has a coat, they both have a coat. I’ve done the maths on fair.  
_Granny Brock goes past with a sack of charcoal, and stops to look at Parcel in its new coat._  
**Granny Brock**: A Nubbin in a coat. Fine. Coat off, when it’s warm. Not the toggles. It likes the toggles.  

### Step 8: Find Parcel. It wasn’t in its basket at bedtime.

Hint: Look for a light next door. Mr Grue keeps a lamp burning in his glasshouse in winter. · check: `find:s28-parcel:glasshouse`

#### Scene `s28-graph`

_Early winter, and frost on Byte’s very straight hedge. He meets you at the door with a sheet of squared paper._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Six jars, one spoon a day. That gets you to midwinter with a spoon to spare. I worked it out the week it came.  
**Byte**: This is what actually happened.  
_The line on the graph goes down like a dropped scarf. In the margin, in capitals: MOSS, ROBINS, YOUR NUBBIN (TWICE), MR GRUE’S STEP???_  
**Byte** _(sad)_: It shares. It feeds everyone before it feeds itself. That isn’t in any book either.  
_You know that rule. You have it in a letter, in the same leaning hand as the tag._  
_On the kitchen table stands the last jar: empty, washed, its lid beside it. Parcel has put it there very neatly._  
**Byte** _(sad)_: It washed it and put it out. Like a milk bottle. In case somebody comes.  
_That night, Parcel isn’t in its basket._  

#### Done `s28-glasshouse`

_At the bottom of Mr Grue’s tidy garden the glasshouse is lit. Steam on the glass, and a lamp held up._  
_Inside, a tree in a tub: dark leaves, and small, rough, bright oranges. The only one in the valley._  
_Parcel is under it with an orange in both paws. Over it stands Mr Grue, with the lamp._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Thief. In my glasshouse. In the night. I’ll have the—  
_Byte comes through the gap in the hedge in his dressing gown and slippers, and steps between them._  
**Byte** _(angry)_: It’s in my care. Anything it’s done is my responsibility. Invoice me.  
_You have never seen Byte stand in front of anything that wasn’t a number. From his face, neither has Byte._  
**Mr Grue** _(think)_: …Those are Seville. Bitter. You can’t eat them raw. It’s in for a nasty surprise.  
**Mr Grue** _(soft)_: My mother brought the pip over the pass in her coat pocket. Grew it in a teacup. Sixty years, and nobody’s ever wanted them.  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Well? What’s on offer? I’m not giving them away.  

**Choices**

- **Promise him the first jar of marmalade.** — sets `{"s28-offer":"jar"}`
    - **You**: The first jar we make. It’s yours.
    - **Mr Grue**: The first jar. Not the second. I’ll know.
    - He puts five oranges in a paper bag, then a sixth, scowling at it as if it went in on its own.
- **Offer to weed his glasshouse for a morning.** — sets `{"s28-offer":"weed"}`
    - **You**: I’ll weed the glasshouse. A whole morning.
    - **Mr Grue**: There aren’t any weeds. …There are some weeds. Saturday. Early.
    - He fills a paper bag with oranges, then holds the lamp for you all the way back to the hedge.
- **Give him a hard stare of your own.** — sets `{"s28-offer":"stare"}`
    - You look at Mr Grue, long and level. You think about lighthouses.
    - **Mr Grue**: Oh, not you as well.
    - He gets to six seconds. Then he pushes a bag of oranges into Byte’s hands and goes in, muttering about the youth of today.

### Step 9: Make marmalade with Byte and Parcel from Mr Grue’s oranges.

Hint: Follow Hazel’s card or Byte’s thermometer: either works. Test the set before you pour it into jars. · check: `craft:s28-marmalade`

#### Scene `s28-kitchen`

_Byte’s kitchen, on a Saturday. Oranges cut, pips in a muslin bag, and the whole house smelling like a brighter house._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Setting point is a hundred and four and a half degrees. I’ve calibrated the thermometer. Twice.  
_Hazel’s recipe card leans on the sugar bag. Under “boil hard” she has written: “Cold saucer, push it, see if it wrinkles.”_  
**Byte** _(think)_: “See if it wrinkles.” That isn’t a test. That’s a feeling.  
_Parcel has put a saucer out on the frosty windowsill. It sits beside it, guarding it, in case someone steals the cold._  
_Byte turns the card over, looking for the rest of the method. On the back, in the same hand, in quotation marks, is one line._  
**Byte** _(think)_: “If we’re kind and polite, the world will be right.”  
**Byte**: That isn’t a recipe. It isn’t even hers. Somebody’s quoting somebody.  
**You**: It’s on the recipe card.  
**Byte** _(think)_: Then it’s an ingredient. Unverified.  
_He props the card back against the sugar bag. Quote side out._  

#### Done `s28-wrinkle`

_The thermometer says a hundred and four and a half. At the same moment, on the cold saucer, the marmalade wrinkles under Parcel’s paw._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: They agree. The feeling and the thermometer agree.  
**Byte** _(think)_: I don’t know how I feel about that.  
_Nine jars glow on the table like a row of small windows. Parcel bows to each one._  
_The first jar goes over the hedge to Mr Grue, promised or not. He takes it at arm’s length._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: I’m taking this as evidence.  
_He doesn’t carry it home at arm’s length. He carries it the way you’d carry an egg._  

### Step 10: Find Parcel in the snow.

Hint: Small prints, close together, going down the lane towards the valley road. Follow them before the snow fills them in. · check: `find:s28-parcel:cart-stop`

#### Scene `s28-first-snow`

_The first snow comes in the night, quietly, the way it always means to._  
_In the morning Byte is at the fence with Mr Grue. They are both pretending to talk about the weather._  
**Mr Grue** _(think)_: So it’s staying, then, your delivery. You’ve verified your sender.  
**Byte**: No. I haven’t. I never asked for it, Mr Grue. It just came. I never asked for it—  
_Behind him, on the step, Parcel puts down its half sandwich. It picks up its basket._  
_By the time Byte has found the end of his sentence, there is nobody on the step to hear it._  
**Mr Grue** _(shock)_: Your… it’s gone. Down the lane. Had that tag held out in front of it, like a ticket.  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: Well, don’t stand there! Go on! It’s snowing!  
**Byte** _(shock)_: I hadn’t finished. I hadn’t FINISHED.  

#### Done `s28-cart-stop`

_The cart stop on the valley road is a sign, a bench and a milestone. The cart doesn’t run in the snow._  
_Parcel is under the sign, very upright, with snow on its head. It is holding its tag out, to be sent back._  
_It sees you both, and bows. It doesn’t put the tag down._  

**Choices**

- **Sit down in the snow beside Parcel.**
    - You sit. The snow is very cold, very fast; Parcel looks at you, then at the road, and doesn’t move away.
    - After a moment Byte sits down on its other side, in the snow, in his good trousers.
    - Parcel unbuttons a toggle and puts half a sandwich in your hand. It has never done that for anyone.
    - **Byte**: …and I’m keeping it.
    - **Byte**: That was the end of the sentence. I never asked for it, and I’m keeping it. You left before the end.
    - He takes the tag, gently, turns it over, and writes on the blank back with the pencil from behind his ear.
    - His own name and his own house, “Next to Mr Grue’s”, and under it, in neat capitals: RECEIVED.
    - Parcel looks at the back of its tag for a long time. Then it bows, and lets Byte carry it home inside his coat.
- **Hang back. Let Byte go.**
    - You stop at the bend. Byte doesn’t: he goes all the way to the sign and crouches in the snow, in his good trousers.
    - **Byte**: …and I’m keeping it.
    - **Byte**: That was the end of the sentence. I never asked for it, and I’m keeping it. You left before the end.
    - He takes the tag, gently, turns it over, and writes on the blank back with the pencil from behind his ear.
    - His own name and his own house, “Next to Mr Grue’s”, and under it, in neat capitals: RECEIVED.
    - Parcel looks at the back of its tag for a long time. Then it bows, and lets Byte carry it home inside his coat.
    - **Byte**: You stayed at the bend. That was right. I’d have been embarrassed.

### Step 11: Follow Parcel up to the cottage.

Hint: Parcel has seen the smoke from Hazel’s chimney. Walk with Byte; it is heading for the bench by the cottage door. · check: `escort:s28-parcel:hazel-bench`

#### Scene `s28-crust`

_The second morning after the Long Night. The Grove is soft with snow, and nobody has slept enough yet._  
_Byte is at your gate, early, with Parcel in its coat and a matchbox in his hand._  
**Byte** _(think)_: I want to tell you something. It isn’t data. I’m going to say it anyway.  
**Byte**: Three hours and twelve minutes in. The third cold. The one where nobody talked.  
**Byte** _(sad)_: My hands were shaking so hard I couldn’t write. I couldn’t write anything down. That has never happened.  
**Byte** _(soft)_: And Parcel came along the line of us, at knee height. With half a sandwich. It gave it to me.  
**Byte** _(soft)_: And my hands stopped. I don’t know why. It doesn’t make sense. I’ve stopped needing it to.  
_He opens the matchbox. Inside, on cotton wool, is a sandwich crust._  
**Byte** _(soft)_: I’ve kept it. Don’t say anything.  
_You don’t. Parcel looks up the lane at Hazel’s chimney, smoking, and sets off without either of you._  

#### Done `s28-bench`

_Hazel is on the bench by the cottage door, in three coats, awake. The thin grey Nubbin sleeps in the crook of her arm._  
_Parcel walks up the path, stops at her boot, and bows. Lower than it has ever bowed to anyone._  
**Gran Hazel** _(soft)_: Hello again, you. Look at that coat. Somebody’s been looking after you.  
_Parcel undoes one toggle, takes out half a sandwich, and holds it up to the thin grey Nubbin._  
_The thin grey Nubbin wakes and sniffs. It takes one corner, very carefully, and eats it._  
**Gran Hazel** _(happy)_: Two days it took me, with an apple. You turn up with a sandwich and do it in one go. Show-off.  

### Step 12: Stay with Byte while he talks to Hazel.

Hint: Sit on the step by the bench. There’s no hurry. · check: `sit:s28-step:hazel`

#### Scene `s28-why`

_Byte has been standing at the gate. Now he comes up the path with his notebook held in front of him, like a ticket._  
**Byte** _(think)_: Hazel. I’ve a theory. Can I say it before you say anything? It’s tidier.  
**Gran Hazel** _(soft)_: Go on, love.  
**Byte** _(think)_: You couldn’t keep it. Not where you were. You knew you might not be there to see it fed.  
**Byte**: So you needed it written down. Every day, every spoon. By somebody who’d notice if a day went missing.  
**Byte**: So you sent it to the one person in the valley who writes everything down.  
_Hazel looks at him for a long time. Then she nods, once._  
**Gran Hazel** _(soft)_: You count things, love. I needed someone who’d count this one.  
_Byte opens his notebook, finds the page, and writes VERIFIED. He doesn’t cross it out._  
_Then he sits down on the step, not suddenly. He has had since the autumn to get used to the idea._  

#### Done `s28-on-the-step`

_You sit on the step beside Byte, with Parcel between you. Nobody says anything for a while, which is the best thing anyone says all morning._  
_Hazel falls asleep in the middle of looking at the three of you. The thin grey Nubbin falls asleep in the middle of her._  
**Byte** _(soft)_: I’m going to throw it a party. A welcome party.  
**Byte** _(think)_: It’s very late for a welcome party. I’ve checked. There’s no rule.  

### Step 13: Get Mr Grue’s watch back together after Parcel’s trick.

Hint: Gather every piece from the napkin, the floor and the jam, and take them to Pixel at the kitchen table. · check: `deliver:s28-watch:pixel`

#### Scene `s28-party`

_Byte’s front room, decorated: bunting cut from squared paper, and a cake with PARCEL on it, iced in very straight capitals._  
**Pixel** _(happy)_: Hi! I brought a cake stand. It rotates. You don’t have to use the rotate.  
_Mr Grue has come “for procedure”, in his best cardigan. He sits on the edge of his chair as if it might go off._  
**Byte** _(think)_: And now. Parcel has prepared a trick. I wasn’t told what. I’m finding that difficult.  
_Parcel walks up to Mr Grue and bows. Then it holds out one paw towards his waistcoat pocket, and waits._  
**Mr Grue** _(think)_: My watch? What does it want my watch for?  
_Everyone looks at him, so he hands the watch over. It was his father’s, he says, twice, on the way._  
_Parcel wraps the watch in a napkin, taps the napkin with a spoon, and unwraps it. The watch has gone._  
_Applause, and a whistle from Pixel. Mr Grue claps three times, against his will._  
_Parcel taps the napkin again and unwraps it. The watch is back, in eleven pieces._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: Eleven.  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: MY FATHER’S WATCH!  
_Mr Grue goes out of the front door so hard that the bunting comes down._  
**Pixel** _(think)_: Give me all the bits. All of them. Nobody sneeze.  

#### Done `s28-grue-back`

_Half an hour later the watch is ticking in Pixel’s palm. It was losing four minutes a day, she says, and it isn’t now._  
_There is a knock. Mr Grue is on the step, holding a paper bag._  
**Mr Grue** _(angry)_: I’ve come for the marmalade. That’s all. Not the party.  
_He holds out the bag. Oranges, small and rough and bright, from the tree that started in a teacup._  
**Mr Grue** _(think)_: For the next lot. You’ll want to start early. Don’t read anything into it.  
_Pixel hands Mr Grue the watch. He holds it to his ear for a long time, then sits down, properly, all the way back in the chair._  

#### Complete `s28-complete`

_When the cake is crumbs, Byte stands up with his notebook, PARCEL: INCIDENTS. It has got quite fat since the autumn._  
**Byte** _(think)_: I’m going to read the log. It’s procedure. Also, I want to.  
**Byte** _(think)_: Incidents. Bath, flood. Fence, Grue. Pyramid, jam. Glasshouse, oranges. Snow. Watch, eleven pieces.  
_Beside his two columns Pixel has added a third, in green pencil, headed NOTES FROM HOME._  
**Pixel** _(happy)_: I annotate his log. He pretends not to read it. He has never once rubbed out a note.  
_Then he turns the notebook sideways. The last column has grown so wide that it runs onto the facing page._  
**Byte** _(soft)_: Good things. Bowed to the kettle. Took Moss elevenses. Shared. Shared. Shared. Washed the jar. Gave me half a sandwich.  
**Byte** _(soft)_: The good column is longer. I measured it. It’s longer by a page and a half.  
_Parcel climbs down off its chair and gives you Hazel’s jar from the Hollow, the empty one, washed. Then it bows._  
_Byte gives your Nubbin a rain hat with room under it for half a sandwich. Parcel already has one: parity._  
_And a luggage tag on string, lettered by Byte in neat capitals: RECEIVED._  
_At the door, Mr Grue gives you a cutting from the orange tree in damp newspaper, and says it probably won’t take._  
_It will. You can tell from how carefully he wrapped it._  

**Rewards**

- Luggage Tag (“Please look after this one”) (cosmetic): A luggage tag on string for your Nubbin, after Parcel’s. Byte lettered yours himself, in neat capitals: RECEIVED.
- Knitted Coat with Toggles (cosmetic): Brown wool, toggles your Nubbin can manage by itself. Byte bought two, for parity. Given at the market (s28-jam-stall).
- Rain Hat with Room for a Sandwich (cosmetic): A wide-brimmed rain hat with a space under the crown for half a sandwich, in case of emergencies.
- Hazel’s Hollow Jar (decor): An empty jar, washed, labelled in Hazel’s hand: “Marmalade. Hollow. Last of.” Put it on a sill and it catches the light.
- Bitter-Orange Cutting (decor): A cutting from Mr Grue’s Seville orange tree, in a pot. He said it wouldn’t take. It takes.
- Byte’s House (feature): Byte’s house becomes a place you can visit, with Parcel living there, Null in the hedge and Mr Grue over the fence.
- Mr Grue and His Orange Tree (feature): Mr Grue joins the valley, with the only bitter-orange tree in it. He will complain about you. He will also give you oranges.
- Marmalade (item): Make it in Byte’s kitchen from Mr Grue’s oranges and Hazel’s recipe card, as often as you like. Nubbins who try it get, alarmingly, quite keen.
- Journal: Parcel, Incidents (feature): Byte’s log becomes a page in your journal, written in his neat capitals, columns and all.

Sets on completion: `{"s28Done":true}`

**Learning Grove swaps**

- `s28-cart#6` → “Please be kind to this small one. It has come a long way.”
- `s28-kitchen#6` → “Be gentle, say thank you, and share what you’ve got.”

**Notes:** THEME. A stranger with a label, taken in by the one Keeper who most needs something he can’t measure. Byte’s whole thread is verifying the sender, and the story ends with him verifying the one thing his columns can’t hold. SIGNATURE CHOICES. (1) s28-told (step 1, s28-for-byte) pays off in step 12: told plays s28-why (Byte’s theory, and he is right); not told plays s28-why-untold (he works out that H. is Hazel there and then, and sits down on the step). (2) s28-column (step 4, s28-eleven-seconds) gets a nod in step 6 (s28-logged / -unverified / -none) and pays off in the complete scene: good = s28-complete, unverified = s28-complete-unverified, none = s28-complete-none. (3) s28-offer (step 8, s28-glasshouse) pays off in step 13’s done: jar = s28-grue-back (a bag of oranges), weed = s28-grue-back-weed (he brings a chair), stare = s28-grue-back-stare (he brings nothing, but stays to the end). Mr Grue gets the first jar in step 9 whatever was offered. VARIANTS (all in SIDE_STORY_S28_VARIANTS). Step 6’s scene: s28-null by default; s28-null-minder replaces it when Pixel’s Minder is still ticking, that is when S7 has reached its Minder (check inspect:device:s7-minder met) and neither s7Done nor s7-stopped is true. If S7 is done, or its lever has been pulled, or S7 never got as far as the Minder, play s28-null (Null playing). Step 6’s done: s28-logged (good), s28-logged-unverified, s28-logged-none, by s28-column. Step 11’s done: s28-bench-bramble replaces s28-bench when s8Reunited is true (colour only). Step 12’s scene: s28-why-untold when s28-told is false. Step 13’s done and the complete scene as above. s28-byte-barks is not a played scene: it is a pool of idle lines for Byte after s28Done (one at random when you talk to him at his house). TIMING. Opens the morning after c8-close: the carrier’s cart stops at the gate (the first story offered that day). Steps 1–7 in autumn, any pace. Step 8 waits for the first winter day on the game clock (or follows step 7 at once if the clock has no seasons). Steps 8–10 must finish before c9-open; the cart-stop and glasshouse scenes sit in early winter so they never clash with S23’s three nights. Step 11 opens the second morning after the Long Night (after c11-open); steps 11–13 fit inside chapter 11’s few days, before c12-open. Step 5’s scene plays at five to eleven in the morning, game time, on the first morning after step 4 (Parcel’s habit is daily; the step is not). CANON. Parcel never speaks; it bows, stares and gestures. Nubbins and animals don’t talk; the robins only eat. Moss takes the Mr Gruber role (elevenses, stories, quiet wisdom). Byte cannot hear Moss until dawn in chapter 10 (c10-moss-stays), so in s28-elevenses Moss speaks to you and Byte reads your face. Moss is kept out of the chapter 11 scenes, so they are neutral to every Moss outcome. Hazel is not seen until c10-close: before then only her handwriting appears (tag, jar label, recipe card), and nothing says where she is or how she is (the ink is old). Steps 11–13 are route-neutral, including keptStone. Hazel speaks only in s28-bench(-bramble) and s28-why(-untold); her line “You count things, love. I needed someone who’d count this one.” is the outline’s, and original. PROPS AND ART. Parcel: a small brown Nubbin, very upright, a basket, a luggage tag on string. No bear features, no duffle coat, no bush hat. Its coat is knitted, with toggles; its rain hat is a sou’wester shape. Mr Grue: cardigan, moustache, slippers; a glasshouse with one bitter-orange tree in a tub; a pocket watch. Byte’s house: a very straight hedge, a gap in it on Mr Grue’s side. The hard stare (step 4): hold on Parcel for a beat with no input; Byte counts under his breath; cut at eleven. Step 10: snow falls throughout; Parcel’s prints are small and close together. MINI-GAME (minigame:clear:s28-mop-up). Mop the water from the bathroom out towards the front door before it reaches the fence. No fail state: if it reaches the fence, it does, and the done scene plays anyway. SIM: (1) Byte’s house becomes a visitable location from step 1 (Parcel lives there as an NPC Nubbin, not ownable). (2) Mr Grue and his glasshouse join the valley at step 3. (3) Optional, no lines: during the vigil (c10-vigil), if step 3 is done, show Parcel beside Byte; this is what step 11 remembers. (4) After s28Done, Byte uses the s28-byte-barks pool at his house. (5) Marmalade (s28-marmalade) is craftable at Byte’s kitchen from Mr Grue’s oranges, unlimited, never required; Nubbins may eat it as a treat. (6) The journal page shows Byte’s log with the columns the player chose (none, good or unverified). (7) Steps 8–10 hold c9-open while S28 is open between step 1 and step 10’s done, using the same backlog rule that holds c5-close for S5 and c11-close for S9. If the player never begins S28 before c9-open, it is withdrawn (the cart does not run in winter). If c12-open arrives before step 13 is done, S28 closes unfinished: the rewards already given stay, s28Done stays unset. LEARNING GROVE. Two childLines replace the lifted lines. The edu version (neighbours, sorry and thank you, kitchen measuring and the cold-saucer test, Seville oranges, what labels and addresses are for, children who travelled with labels) is a separate pass.

## S29. The Gates of Dawn

*Kenneth Grahame, The Wind in the Willows (Methuen, 1908; public domain): the river, the Wild Wood, Badger’s kitchen, Dulce Domum, The Piper at the Gates of Dawn and Wayfarers All* · audience: all · where: The stream below the Grove; later Marina’s river beyond the ridge, the Wild Wood under the Circuit and Dusty’s cliff · when: Opens after c4-close (the week after Lantern Night). Steps are gated by chapter (see notes) and run to chapter 7. Closes when chapter 8 opens.

**Cast:** Granny Brock (Mr Badger (Kenneth Grahame, The Wind in the Willows, 1908), recast as an old charcoal-burner), The seafarer (the Sea Rat ("Wayfarers All", The Wind in the Willows, 1908), recast as a person), Valley children (the field-mice carol singers ("Dulce Domum", The Wind in the Willows, 1908))

### Step 1: Help Marina carry her mended boat down to the water.

Hint: The boat is upside down on the bank below the Grove. Take one end; Marina takes the other, and tells you which is which. · check: `carry:s29-boat:stream`

#### Scene `s29-digging`

_The week after Lantern Night. Down by the stream the air smells of cut grass and warm mud._  
_Marina is on the bank with her boat upside down on two trestles, painting its bottom blue and humming._  
_A few yards off, the turf heaves. It heaves again and splits, and a pair of earthy hands comes up through it._  
_Then a head, covered in soil, blinking at the sun as if the sun had done it on purpose._  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: Bother! O blow!  
_Dusty climbs out, shakes off about a bucketful of earth, and looks back down the hole with real dislike._  
**Dusty**: I’ve been clearing out the burrow since March. Every time I finish a room, the room next door falls in.  
**Dusty** _(angry)_: Hang digging!  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Well. Nobody in this valley ever thought they’d hear you say that.  
**Dusty**: Nobody in this valley ever heard me mean it. Morning. What are you doing to that boat?  
**Marina** _(think)_: Mending her. She’s done. Come on, both of you, one at each end. You’ve never been in a boat, have you, Dusty?  
**Dusty**: I’ve been in a hole. Is it much different?  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Opposite, mostly.  

#### Done `s29-launch`

_The boat goes into the stream with a smack and a wobble, and sits there looking pleased with itself._  
_Your Nubbin is in before anyone else, and takes the very front: the little pointed seat._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: That’s the bow. And that’s a Nubbin who knows what’s what.  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: It’s moving. It’s just sitting there, and it’s moving. Is it meant to do that?  
**Marina**: That’s the whole idea, love.  

### Step 2: Go down the stream with Marina to the backwater, and have the picnic.

Hint: Sit still in the middle while Marina rows. Leaning over the side to look at fish does not count as sitting still. · check: `explore:s29-river:backwater`

#### Scene `s29-boats`

**Marina** _(think)_: Vests on. Cork, see? Nobody gets in my boat without one. Not you, not Dusty, not me, not the King.  
_The vests are old and stiff, and smell faintly of the sea._  
_Dusty puts theirs on back to front, then the right way round, then back to front again._  
_Marina pushes off. The bank slides away, and the stream takes the boat as if it had been waiting all morning._  
**Dusty** _(think)_: So what is it you actually do? In a boat? All day?  
_Marina stops rowing. She looks at Dusty very seriously, as if she has waited years for someone to ask._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Believe me, my young friend, there is nothing—absolute nothing—half so much worth doing as simply messing about in boats.  
**Dusty**: That’s not an answer.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: It’s the only answer. Duck. Low branch.  
_In the bow, your Nubbin leans out over the water with its ears blown flat, like a figurehead that has seen something wonderful._  
_Dusty tries to swap seats with it, once. It gives them a look, and they sit back down._  

#### Done `s29-picnic`

_The stream runs into the river, and the river turns off into a backwater: still and green, with a willow leaning over it._  
_Marina ties up to the willow, hauls out a wicker basket, and starts unpacking it onto a blue-and-white rug._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Cold chicken. Ham. Two loaves, one for now and one for later. Three cheeses. Gherkins. Seed cake. Ginger beer. Lemonade…  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: O stop, stop! This is too much!  
**Marina**: It’s a picnic, Dusty. Too much is the amount.  
_Out in the middle of the backwater a line of bubbles comes up. Then a flat brown head, whiskered, watching you._  
_Then a second head beside it, much smaller and much rounder. It isn’t watching you at all; it is watching the gherkins._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Otters. Keep still. That’s the old one, and that’s his kit. I call him Portly. You’ll see why.  
_The old otter looks at you a long time, and sinks without a ripple. The kit doesn’t sink; the kit comes ashore._  
_He walks straight up the rug, takes the biggest gherkin from the jar, and goes, dripping, before anyone can move._  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: He took a gherkin. He looked me in the eye and he took it.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: He does that. Last summer it was a whole pork pie. He couldn’t lift it. He tried for an hour.  

### Step 3: Fish the picnic out of the river and get everyone back to the bank.

Hint: Your Nubbins can swim; let them. Grab whatever floats: the basket, the rug, the oars. Leave the gherkins to the otters. · check: `collect:s29-picnic:river`

#### Scene `s29-oars`

_After lunch, with the sun gone soft and the basket much lighter, Marina rows you back out of the backwater._  
**Dusty**: Let me have a go.  
**Marina** _(think)_: Not today. Next time, when you’ve watched a bit more.  
**Dusty**: I’ve watched loads. You just pull. Anyone can pull. I pull roots out of cliffs.  
_Before she can stop them, Dusty has grabbed both oars, stood up, and dug them hard into the river._  
_One oar bites. The other skims the top of the water and sends Dusty over backwards, boots in the air._  
**Marina** _(shock)_: You’ve caught a crab. That’s what it’s called. Sit down. SIT—  
_Dusty sits up, grabs the side, and the whole boat turns over, quite slowly, as if it has thought about it first._  
_…_  
_Green water, bubbles, a great deal of noise. Then the cork vests do exactly what they’re for, and you bob up like three corks._  
_Your Nubbins paddle past you towards the bank in a neat line, unbothered, as if they’d planned to swim home all along._  

#### Done `s29-fire`

_Marina gets everything ashore: you, Dusty, the basket, the rug, both oars. Then she swims back for the boat._  
_She lights a fire in a ring of stones and sits Dusty in front of it, wrapped in the rug._  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: I’m so sorry. Your boat. Your lovely picnic. I’m an idiot.  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Never mind. Honestly. Never mind.  
**Dusty**: The cheese is in the river.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Never mind the cheese. The fish will have a lovely afternoon. Never mind.  
_She says it until Dusty believes it. It takes most of an hour, and a whole pot of tea._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Tomorrow I’ll teach you properly. Both of you. Then at least you’ll fall in the right way.  

### Step 4: Take Marina’s rowing lesson, then find something to give Portly.

Hint: Pull with your back, not your arms, and look where you’ve been, not where you’re going. Portly will be close by. He always is. · check: `give:s29-portly:gift`

#### Scene `s29-lessons`

**Marina** _(think)_: Lesson one: sit down. Lesson two: stay sat down. Most people never get past two.  
**Marina**: Feet braced. Pull with your back. Blades in, pull, out, and turn them flat. Look behind you. Rowing is all looking behind you.  
**Dusty**: That seems like a design fault.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Take it up with the river.  
_By the afternoon you can both go in a straight line, mostly. Dusty only catches one more crab, and keeps their seat._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: And one for the little one. Cut it down from an old one. Arms up.  
_Marina buckles a tiny cork vest onto your Nubbin. It stands very still, deeply proud, then marches up and down so everyone can see._  
_All afternoon there has been a small brown head in the water behind the boat. Wherever you row, it rows too, more or less._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Portly. He’s adopted you. He does that, then he forgets, then he does it again.  
**Marina**: Give him something, if you like. Not food; he’ll only want more food. Something he can keep.  

**Choices**

- **A blue glass bead from your pocket.** — sets `{"s29-gift":"bead"}`
    - You hold it out on a flat palm. Portly comes up the bank, sniffs it, and takes it in his teeth very carefully, like an egg.
    - He rolls it about on a flat stone for ten minutes, watching the light go through it.
    - **Marina**: Magpie. He’ll have that till he’s grey.
- **A red ribbon off the picnic basket.** — sets `{"s29-gift":"ribbon"}`
    - You lay it on the bank. Portly takes one end and swims off with it trailing behind him like a flag.
    - **Dusty**: Well, we’ll see him coming a mile off now.
    - **Marina**: Half a mile. That’ll do.
- **A tiny brass bell off an old collar.** — sets `{"s29-gift":"bell"}`
    - Marina threads it on a loop of soft cord, loose enough to slip off if it catches. Portly lets you put it over his head.
    - He shakes, and it rings. He shakes again, astonished, and then can’t stop.
    - **Marina**: Well. We’ll always know where he is now. Whether we want to or not.

#### Done `s29-lessons-done`

_Portly takes his present home along the bank in the evening light, very slowly, stopping to look at it._  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: He’s never stolen anything off you. Have you noticed? That’s how you can tell he likes you.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Same time next week. If either of you stands up in my boat again, I’ll sink you myself.  

### Step 5: Go into the Wild Wood with Marina and find Dusty.

Hint: Keep to the path and keep talking. If you lose the path, stand still and listen for Dusty instead. · check: `find:s29-dusty:wild-wood`

#### Scene `s29-wildwood`

_August, and there is frost on the grass at dusk, and nobody can say why. It seems to come down off the slope below the Circuit._  
_Marina is at your gate with a lantern and a stout stick. She doesn’t lift and push; she shouts._  
**Marina** _(angry)_: Dusty’s gone into the Wild Wood. On their own. At teatime. They’ve been like a cat in a thunderstorm all week.  
**Marina**: I said don’t, it’s getting dark. They said they only wanted a look. Nobody only has a look in the Wild Wood.  
_Later, Dusty will tell it like this, and nobody will laugh._  
_It was quiet under the first trees, and cold. The path went in, the light went out, and the frost crunched._  
_Then the faces began._  
_Little ones, in the holes in the banks and between the roots. Gone whenever you looked straight at them._  
_Then the pattering: behind, ahead, either side, like rain that hadn’t decided where to fall._  
_Dusty ran, which is the wrong thing. They ran until they fell into the hollow of an old beech, and stayed there._  
**Marina** _(think)_: Rules of the Wood. Keep to the path. Don’t run. Keep talking. If you run out of things to say, say anything.  
**Marina**: Shopping lists. Times tables. Anything. Come on.  

#### Done `s29-beech`

_You go in talking. Marina names every rock pool on the coast from west to east, and you do your times tables, badly._  
**Marina** _(think)_: Seven eights?  
_“Fifty-four?” you say._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Keep going. The Wood can’t tell.  
_The faces, if they are faces, keep to the edge of the lantern light. The pattering keeps pace with you, and stops when you stop._  
_Your Nubbin rides inside your coat with only its eyes showing. It doesn’t like the Wood, and it doesn’t make a sound._  
_At last, in the hollow of a great beech, something says “Ow” very quietly. Dusty is curled in the dead leaves, white with frost._  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: I only wanted a look.  
**Dusty**: I know. I know. Don’t say it.  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Wasn’t going to. You’re frozen. Up you get.  
_But the frost has thickened while you searched. The path has gone under it, and the lantern is guttering._  
**Marina** _(think)_: We’re not getting home tonight. Not that way.  

### Step 6: Find shelter in the Wild Wood before the lantern goes out.

Hint: Look for anything people made: a step, a scraper, a door. Old stones mean old houses. · check: `find:s29-brock-door:wild-wood`

#### Scene `s29-door`

_You go on slowly, holding on to one another’s sleeves, with Dusty in the middle._  
_Dusty trips over something, hard, and sits down in the frost holding their shin._  
**Dusty** _(angry)_: Ow. Something with an edge. In the middle of a wood. Who leaves an edge in a wood?  
_Marina scrapes the frost away with her stick. It is an iron boot-scraper, very old, set in a stone step._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: A scraper means a step. A step means a door. Dig, Dusty. You’re good at that.  
**Dusty**: Fine. Yes. Digging. I take it all back.  
_Under the frost there is a doormat, and beyond the doormat a little green door set into a bank under old stones._  
_Marina finds a bell-pull and pulls it. Somewhere deep in the bank, a long way off, a bell clanks._  
_Nothing. Then, very slowly, bolts going back: one, two, three, and a fourth, rusty, that takes some persuading._  

#### Done `s29-kitchen`

_The door opens a crack. An old woman with a candle glares out: a quilted dressing gown, and a soot-black apron over it._  
**Granny Brock** _(angry)_: Who’s ringing my bell at this hour? Nobody rings it at any hour. That’s why it’s there.  
**Marina**: Granny Brock. It’s Marina, from the river. We’re lost, and this one’s frozen.  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: Marina. Hm. And a frozen one. And somebody’s grandchild, by the chin on it. And a… what is that, in your coat?  
_Your Nubbin looks out at her, and she looks back. Neither of them blinks first, and something is settled._  
**Granny Brock**: In. All of you. Boots off. Not you, you’ve not got boots. In.  
_The passage runs back into the bank and opens into a kitchen older than the Wood: stone flags, a red fire, hams on hooks._  
_The walls are carved with bits of words nobody can read now. It is the ruin of a house older than anyone’s grandmother._  
**Granny Brock**: Sit. Eat. Then talk, if you must. Better if you mustn’t.  
_She feeds you soup, and bread, and more soup. She puts Dusty nearest the fire and drops a blanket on them without looking._  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: The Wood? It looks. That’s all it does. It looks at folk who don’t belong to it, and they run, and that’s their lookout.  
**Granny Brock**: My people burned charcoal in this wood before your grandmothers were born. There was a town here once. Now there’s me.  
**Granny Brock**: People come and go. The Wood stays. So do I.  
_Dusty listens to every word, very hard, the way you listen to someone old when someone at home is getting old too._  
_When the fire gets low, Granny Brock puts another log on without being asked. Nobody goes to sleep for a long time._  

### Step 7: Follow Granny Brock out of the Wood the back way.

Hint: Keep one hand on the wall and your head down. The old culvert comes out at the edge of the fields. · check: `walk:s29-culvert:fields`

#### Scene `s29-culvert`

_First light. Granny Brock is up before any of you, in boots and a coat older than her door._  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: Not back through the Wood. Not with that one. The Wood’s had its look at them already.  
**Granny Brock**: There’s an old way. A drain from the old town. Goes under the lot and comes out by the fields. Mind your heads.  
_She takes a lantern and a ring of keys off a nail and leads you down a passage, then down some steps, then down some more._  
_The culvert is low and round and dry, made of old brick. Your Nubbin runs ahead into the dark, then runs back to check you’re coming._  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: That one’s sensible. Goes ahead, comes back. More than I can say for some.  

#### Done `s29-fields`

_Daylight, round and white at the end of the tunnel. You come out among nettles at the edge of a hayfield, blinking._  
_Behind you the Wood stands dark and quiet, and doesn’t look at all as if it was looking at anyone._  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: Go home. Don’t come in after dark. Come in the day, if you like. Bring cake. Don’t ring the bell.  
_She goes back into the culvert and the dark takes her. Somewhere inside, a gate shuts, and four bolts._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: I’m a river person now. I’ve decided. Rivers are flat. You can see what’s coming. Nothing looks at you from a river.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Otters do.  
**Dusty**: Otters are allowed.  

### Step 8: Row up the river to the weir pool with Marina and Dusty.

Hint: Take an oar each. Above the backwater the river narrows; where it’s too shallow to row, everyone gets out and carries. · check: `minigame:s29-rowing:weir`

#### Scene `s29-low-river`

_Summer’s end. Since the rain on the hill, Dusty has been sleeping in Marina’s boathouse, and nobody has said a word about it._  
_Marina wants to show you the top of the river before the weather turns. Dusty takes an oar without being asked, and sits down first._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Look at that. Sitting down. I’ve made a sailor of you.  
_Above the backwater the river is wrong. The banks stand high and dry, and weed lies flat on stones that ought to be under water._  
**Marina** _(think)_: A hand too low. It’s been a hand too low all summer. Something upstream’s having it.  
_Halfway up, the boat grounds on gravel. There is nothing for it: out, all of you, and carry._  
_The water round your ankles is cold enough to make your teeth hurt._  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: Cold. In August.  
**Marina** _(sad)_: It was cold in July, too. Nobody listened then either.  

#### Done `s29-weir`

_At the top, the river comes over the weir in one long glassy fold, and roars, and spreads into a wide brown pool below._  
_Just above the weir, where the river splits to go round it, there is an island: willows, and a little bank of long grass._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Nobody lands there.  
**Dusty**: Why not?  
**Marina** _(think)_: Don’t know. Nobody does. My gran didn’t, and her gran didn’t. There’s nothing on it but grass.  
**Marina**: There’s a spring on it, warm all winter. Same water as behind the falls, my gran reckoned.  
**Marina**: Come on, it’s getting on. We’ll leave her at the landing under the cliff and walk the last bit.  

### Step 9: Catch Marina up on the river path and tell her what’s wrong.

Hint: She’s gone on ahead towards the boathouse. She walks fast when she’s hungry. Run. · check: `talk:marina:s29-dusk-path`

#### Scene `s29-dusk`

_Dusk along the river path. Above you rises a cliff striped like a cake: brown, red, grey, brown._  
_Marina walks ahead, fast, talking about supper. Halfway along, Dusty stops dead, as if somebody has called their name._  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: Oh.  
_They are looking up the cliff, at a small round door in the rock and a window with no light in it._  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: That’s mine. I can smell it. Earth, and old tea, and… it’s mine. I haven’t been back. Not since the rain.  
**Dusty**: Marina! Marina, wait—  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Come on, you two! Supper! I’m starving, and it’s getting dark!  
_Marina hasn’t heard; she’s already round the bend. Dusty looks after Marina, and up at the door, and after her again._  
_Then they sit down on an old stump by the path, put their face in their hands, and cry._  

#### Done `s29-turnback`

_You catch Marina up at the bend. You don’t have to say much: you say “Dusty”, and point._  
_She turns round at once, in the middle of a word, and goes back down the path at a run._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Dusty. Oh, Dusty. What is it?  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: It’s the house. It’s only the house. I can’t go in. Everything in it is hers.  
**Dusty**: And I can’t walk past it either. I tried. My feet won’t.  
**Marina** _(sad)_: Then we won’t walk past it. I’m an idiot. I was thinking about my supper.  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Right. Up we go. All of us. I’m not leaving you on a stump.  

### Step 10: Fetch dry kindling for Dusty’s stove.

Hint: The woodpile is round the side of the cliff house. The top layer is wet; dig down. · check: `collect:s29-kindling:cliff-house`

#### Scene `s29-cliffhouse`

_Inside, Dusty’s house is cold, dusty and dark: a round room cut from the rock, a stove, two chairs and a great many trowels._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Look at it. A proper little house, this. Snug as an egg. And you’ve been sleeping in my boathouse with the nets.  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: It’s not snug. It’s cold. There’s nothing to eat.  
**Marina** _(think)_: There’s always something to eat. You: kindling, the dry stuff, round the side. Dusty: sit. I’ll do the cupboards.  
_Dusty sits. Marina goes through the cupboards like weather, opening everything and talking to all of it._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Biscuits. Ancient. They’ll do. Tea. A jar of… pickled onions? They’ll do. Candles!  

#### Done `s29-supper`

_You come back with your arms full. Soon the stove is roaring, the room has gone gold, and even the dust looks cosy._  
_Marina lays out biscuits, onions and tea on an upturned crate as if it were a banquet. Your Nubbin sits as close to the stove as it dares._  
_Beside the stove there is a round basket with a cushion in it, flattened in the middle. Nobody sits on that side of the stove._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: Fossil’s. She liked it there. Twenty years by that stove, and she never once let me have the warm side.  
**Dusty**: I couldn’t come in with it there. And I couldn’t move it. So I didn’t come in.  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Leave it where it is. It’s hers. It can be hers and still be in your house.  
_Outside: feet on the cliff path, whispering, and a great deal of shushing. A lantern on a pole bobs up past the window._  
_Dusty opens the door. Half the valley’s children are on the step in their wellies, red-nosed, holding the lantern up high._  
**Valley children**: We heard you were home. It’s not Lantern Night, we know. We practised one verse anyway.  
**Valley children** _(soft)_: Light one for the ones who went up the rise  
**Valley children** _(soft)_: with the morning sun in their sleeping eyes;  
**Valley children** _(soft)_: we are made of the ones who remember us here,  
**Valley children** _(soft)_: so we gather, we gather, we gather each year.  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: …Thank you. Thank you. Come in, all of you. There’s biscuits. They’re very old.  
_That night Dusty sleeps at home, in their own bed, with the stove ticking and the basket where it always was._  

### Step 11: Go down to the ford at dusk and see the old otter.

Hint: The ford is just below the backwater, where the stepping stones cross. Go quietly, and not too close. · check: `watch:s29-old-otter:ford`

#### Scene `s29-missing`

_The last days of summer. The swallows are lining up on the wires, and the evenings smell of smoke._  
_Marina comes up the lane early, without her boat, without a basket, and without a joke._  
**Marina** _(sad)_: Portly’s gone. Three days now. Nobody’s seen him on the river, up or down.  
**Marina**: He wanders. He always has, and he’s always turned up. But never this long, and the nights are getting cold.  
**Marina** _(sad)_: His dad won’t make a fuss. Otters don’t. That’s how you know it’s bad.  
**You**: Where does the old one go?  
**Marina**: The ford. Where he taught Portly to swim. Come and see.  

#### Done `s29-ford-watch`

_At the ford the water runs shallow and loud over the stepping stones. On the flattest stone sits the old otter, quite still._  
_He doesn’t look at you. He looks upstream, the way the water comes, and doesn’t move, and doesn’t eat._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Every night, and every morning before it’s light, he sits right there. Waiting for the water to bring him back.  
_Dusty comes down the bank behind you with a flask and a blanket. They sit a little way off from the old otter, and stay._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: Somebody ought to sit with him. I know about sitting.  
_Dusty pours soup into the flask lid and offers it to the otter._  
_The otter looks at the soup, then at Dusty. Dusty drinks it._  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: I didn’t know what otters drink. It turns out it’s soup, and it turns out it’s me.  
**Marina** _(think)_: Tonight we go up. You and me, in the boat, all the way to the weir if we have to.  
**Marina**: Bring a lantern. And bring that one of yours. They see better in the dark than we do.  

### Step 12: Row up the river in the dark and look for Portly.

Hint: Row slowly and keep the lantern low over the water. Watch the banks, the reeds and the mud, and listen. · check: `minigame:s29-rowing:night`

#### Scene `s29-upriver`

_Night. The river is black and smooth, and the lantern makes a small gold room on it that goes where you go._  
_Marina rows. You hold the lantern out over the bow, where your Nubbin sits in its cork vest with its ears up._  
**Marina** _(think)_: Keep it low. Look at the edges: mud, reeds, roots. He’ll be somewhere a kit thinks is a good idea.  
_Owls, a fish jumping, once a dog far off. The river makes small noises, and every one of them is not Portly._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: He’s a good swimmer. I taught him. Well. He taught himself, and I watched and said well done.  
**Marina**: Vest done up? Right. We go on till we find him or the river runs out.  

#### Done `s29-night`

_Something winks in the lantern beam, down on a mudbank. Your Nubbin sees it first, and nearly goes over the side._  
_It’s the blue glass bead, pressed into the mud. Round it, small webbed prints lead off upstream._  
**Marina** _(shock)_: He was here. He put it down. He never puts it down.  
_You put the bead in your pocket and row on, following the prints until the mud runs out at the weir._  
_You haul the boat up the old slipway beside the weir and put her in again above it. Up here the water is very still._  
_Then you hear it. Not music, exactly._  
_Like wind in reeds, if wind could mean something. Like the weir, if the weir could sing._  
_Marina stops rowing. The oars drip, and she doesn’t notice._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Do you hear that?  
_Your Nubbin is standing up in the bow, facing upstream. It isn’t frightened; it is listening with its whole body._  
_Ahead, where the river splits to go round the island, the sky has gone the colour of the inside of a shell. It is nearly dawn._  

### Step 13: Row Portly home to the ford.

Hint: Downstream all the way. Let the river do the work, and keep him warm. · check: `deliver:s29-portly:ford`

#### Scene `s29-island`

_Marina rows on, not as if she has decided to. The boat touches the island with a soft sound, and you step out._  
_The air is warm, warmer than any night this summer. It smells of wet stone, deep water and something green._  
_Among the willows there is a hollow in the long grass, pressed flat, as if something large had sat there all night._  
_In the middle of it, curled nose to tail, is Portly. Asleep, round and safe, his sides going up and down._  
_Your Nubbin walks to the edge of the hollow and stops. The ball above its head begins to glow, softly, the colour of the sky._
