    - His sign stays by the long grass all winter, and nobody moves it. The frost comes to it every morning, and goes again.
- **“Yes. And I’m bringing my own blanket.”**
    - **You**: Yes. And I’m bringing my own blanket.
    - **Tam**: That’s fair. The spare one’s his.
    - His sign stays by the long grass all winter, and nobody moves it. The frost comes to it every morning, and goes again.

#### Complete `s31-complete`

_Next morning, Tam is down at the long grass before anyone else is up. By the foot of his sign, someone has left something._  
_One acorn. Just one, very round and very shiny, set exactly in front of the sign._  
**Tam** _(happy)_: It’s a toy. A medium toy. He read the letter.  
**Tam** _(happy)_: He couldn’t come, so he sent a deposit.  
_Up on the hill, Moss is sitting very still, with the look of someone who has never in his life been near an acorn._  
_Sparks won’t take the goggles back. She says they’ve seen action now, and it would be wrong._  
_Your gourd lantern goes on the wall by the gate, where it can be seen from the air. Its face is still mostly thinking about smiling._  

**Rewards**

- Gourd lantern (decor): The small, lopsided gourd you carved with Tam: round eyes, no teeth, a mouth mostly thinking about smiling. It glows by the gate after dark.
- Flying-ace goggles and scarf (cosmetic): Sparks’s old welding goggles and a long red scarf that goes round twice and still trails. A Nubbin wearing them looks at the sky as if it owes them money.
- Ned’s rock (item): A keepsake. A plain grey rock with a paper label in Byte’s small square capitals: BASALT. Given to you for being the witness.
- Tam’s sign (decor): WELCOME GREAT GOURD, painted on a tea tray, R backwards for luck. It stands by the long grass until spring; then Tam lets you keep it.

Sets on completion: `{"s31Done":true}`

**Learning Grove swaps**

- `s31-creed#8` → There are three things I never argue about, and the Great Gourd is all three of them.
- `s31-rock#1` → Another stone. I’m starting to think it’s me.
- `s31-next-year#8` → And anyway, Nettie’s crosser than anyone. She missed all the guising, waiting with me.

**Notes:** THEME. Rule three as a whole story: the grown-up who stays. Tam is the hero; the Keeper is a guest. The story never says whether the Great Gourd is real, and nobody wins by mocking Tam. Ivy is prickly, not cruel: she carries him home. THE THIRD RULE is named exactly once, in s31-long-grass’s sit choice (canon 14). Nowhere else in S31 may name or quote it. SIGNATURE CHOICE. Step 8 (s31-night = guise | party | sat) pays off in steps 11–14: who is there at midnight (step 11), who rises out of the frost and whether Sable comes (step 12), Ivy’s blanket (step 13) and Tam’s ending (step 14). Step 1’s letter (s31-letter) leaves its trace in the complete scene, thirteen steps later. TIMING. Opens after c7-stillness, at the Grove wall. Steps 1–6 over the following days (step 4 is “next morning”). Gourd Night is the first dusk after step 6’s done (s31-moss): s31-dusk then plays at dusk. Steps 7–13 run in that one night; step 14 plays at lunchtime next day, and the complete scene the morning after. If chapter 8 opens before Gourd Night, the story closes unfinished: no rewards, s31Done stays unset. A step’s scene plays when the previous check is met; its done plays when its own check is met. Checks: puzzle (spelling), visit, craft (carving), watch, talk, build (knock the sign in), place (dress), choose (step 8’s choice itself), talk:s31-ned:rock (met when Ned shows the rock, in any branch), minigame (the flying ace: guide your Nubbin along hedges, away from the frosty grass, to the workshop; no fail state), and the three waits (midnight, frost, first light), which are short skippable night waits with the clock shown, no fail state. VARIANTS (all in SIDE_STORY_S31_VARIANTS). Step 9 scene: s31-guising (default) when s31-night = guise; s31-guising-sat when sat (Ned comes back past the long grass); s31-guising-party when party (the guisers arrive at the party). The done s31-rock is shared. Step 10 is shared by every branch (your Nubbin slips away from you at dusk whatever you chose). Step 11 scene: s31-if (default) when sat; s31-if-away when guise or party (narrated without you; Luna’s lantern and Clover’s lamp keep watch). Step 11 done: s31-midnight (default); s31-midnight-wisp instead when wisp = spared or sable at that moment (either step 11 scene). Step 12 scene s31-frost is shared. Step 12 done: s31-shape (default) when sat and s31-sable30 is false; s31-shape-sable when sat and s31-sable30 is true; s31-shape-away when guise or party. Step 13 scene: s31-four (default) when sat and no Sable; s31-four-sable when s31-shape-sable played; s31-four-away when guise or party (no blanket). Step 13 done: s31-light (default); s31-light-sable when s31-shape-sable played. Step 14 scene s31-next-year is shared. Step 14 done: s31-promise (default) when sat; s31-promise-went when guise or party (the second sign, SEAT FOR A KEEPER (IF), appears next morning; keep it beside the first all winter). Complete: s31-complete (default) when s31-letter = toys (one acorn); s31-complete-strays when strays (a heap of fruit, which you put out at the edge of the Grove); s31-complete-busy when busy (nothing, which Tam takes as proof). SABLE. She comes only if you sat (s31-night = sat) AND her trust was 30 or more at the start of Gourd Night (s31-sable30). She is narrated, never a speaker, and never in a choice. S31 never changes her trust. WISP. Plays only if S2 has been decided as spared or sable before midnight on Gourd Night. Often it will not have been (S2 decides at the end of chapter 7); then s31-midnight plays. Wisp says nothing here. AFTER THE STORY (Tier 2). Tam, Ivy, Ned and Nettie become valley children in the lane with ambient lines (s31-ambient-tam, -ivy, -ned, -nettie; any time after s31Done, not during chapters 9–10’s night scenes). Tam’s sign stands by the long grass (by the fourth post, outside the frost decal) until spring, then becomes the s31-sign decor. After the chapter 10 dawn, if keptStone is false (the Long-Grass lantern lit itself), s31-ambient-lantern replaces s31-ambient-tam. The gourd lantern decor is placeable from step 3 (s31-kinder) on; the goggles from step 7. SIM: (1) snapshot Sable’s trust when s31-dusk begins and set s31-sable30 = (trust >= 30); (2) do not queue c7-close or open chapter 8 while Gourd Night is running (s31-dusk begun, step 13 not done); (3) during steps 8–13 keep all Nubbins out of the frost decal except Sable (in s31-shape-sable); (4) Gourd Night staging: lit gourds on the lane steps, Clover’s lamp lit, the three lanterns lit and the fourth dark; (5) new decor/cosmetic/item ids as in rewards. ART. Tam: seven, patchwork quilt always, trailing to his boots. Ivy: ten, straight fringe, clipboard; witch’s hat and broom on Gourd Night. Ned: nine, round worried face; ghost sheet with far too many eyeholes. Nettie: six, small, one boot off by the small hours. Your Nubbin: welding goggles on top, red scarf twice round. Sign: tea tray, R backwards. SOUND. Pixel’s pianola plays original tunes only (Schroeder’s music is not used). Nobody sings. One accidental howl.

#### Variant `s23-note-bargained`

**Corvin Vale**: “Something came last night. I’d like it seen by someone who isn’t me. C.V.”  
_On the back, smaller, as if it were added at the door: “Dusk.”_  
_Next door, Fern is pretending to prune something over the fence, in December, when there is nothing to prune._  
**Fern** _(shock)_: Is that from him? Up the valley? Don’t go.  
**Fern** _(think)_: …You’re going. You’ve got the chin out. Take a scarf. Take two.

#### Variant `s23-caller-aldous`

_Midnight. The clock strikes, and the nailed door doesn’t open, and someone comes in through it anyway._  
_A big man in a miller’s apron, gold at the edges the way old memories are. You can see the nails of the door through him._  
_He is wound round and round with chain: the mill’s drive chain, black and heavy, dragging on the boards behind him._  
**Corvin Vale** _(shock)_: …Father.  
_Aldous Vale looks at his son. Then at the clock, out of habit, as if checking the shift._  
**Corvin Vale** _(think)_: That’s the drive chain. From the wheel. Why are you—  
**Aldous Vale**: I wear the chain I forged in life. I made it link by link, and yard by yard.  
**Aldous Vale**: Three will come, boy. One a night. Sit up for them.  
**Aldous Vale** _(soft)_: Don’t count the hours. I counted the hours.  
_He looks at you in the second chair, as if making sure there is a witness. Then the chain draws him back through the door._  
_Corvin takes out a ledger and a pencil, and writes down the time. Under it, in the same square hand: “Nothing felt.”_  
**Corvin Vale**: Thank you. You’ve witnessed it. You can go home now.  
_He says it kindly, and he means it. It is the first thing he has asked of you that isn’t a price._  

**Choices**

- **Go out, and sit on his step instead.** — sets `{"s23-stay":"step"}`
    - You go out and sit on his front step in the frost, with your back to his door.
    - After a while the latch clicks behind you: not opening, just checking that you’re there.
    - Inside, the clock begins to strike one.
- **Go home, as he asks.** — sets `{"s23-stay":"home"}`
    - You go. The lane is white and silent, and your breath walks on ahead of you.
    - At the bend you look back. One window is lit, and inside, very faintly, a clock begins to strike one.
- **Tell him flatly you’re staying.** — sets `{"s23-stay":"insist"}`
    - **You**: I’m staying.
    - **Corvin Vale**: …I see.
    - He looks at you for a long moment, like a sum that won’t come out. Then he gets up and fills the kettle.
    - The clock begins to strike one.

#### Variant `s23-briar-collector`

_One o’clock. A small warm light comes up the lane, bobbing, with a laugh in it somewhere._  
_It doesn’t matter where you were: lane, step or chair. When it comes in at the nailed door, you are in the room to meet it._  
_It is a Nubbin, or the shape of one, made of morning. Light springs up off the crown of her head like water from a spring._  
**Corvin Vale**: Who are you?  
**Briar** _(happy)_: Briar. What was.  
**Corvin Vale** _(think)_: Long ago?  
**Briar**: No. Your past.  
_She laughs, not unkindly: the laugh of someone who has just noticed how serious everybody is being._  
**Corvin Vale**: You’re the one in the song. The stream lantern. You’ve been gone longer than this cottage has stood.  
**Briar** _(happy)_: Gone. Yes. Up you get, Corvin Vale.  
**Briar**: And you. Partner.  
_Her light lands on you, and for a moment you are at your own gate in early summer, with two hundred Shells in your hand._  
_Then it moves on, and you aren’t._  
**Briar** _(soft)_: Yours later. His first.  
_She takes his hand, and his hand doesn’t notice. Her other paw she holds out to you, and it is like holding a patch of sun._  
_The back wall of the cottage goes thin as paper, and then it isn’t there at all._

**Corvin Vale** _(soft)_: I don’t know why I made it. I think I wanted there to be two.

#### Variant `c10-vigil-corvin`

**Clover** _(soft)_: He stayed till first light. Then he went home.

#### Variant `s24-tea-moss`

_Spring. The memorial flowers on the hill have opened all at once, as if somebody gave a signal._  
_Hazel’s is the untidy one near Briar’s. It still faces the Grove, very firmly._  
_Somebody comes over the far ridge in the early light, under a pack so big it looks like a walking hedge._

**Nova** _(think)_: I thought I’d sit here a minute and not pass.

**Moss** _(think)_: I’m coming. Wherever this is going.

**Nova** _(soft)_: Is there tea? There’s usually tea. I usually say no.  
**You**: There’s tea.  
**Nova**: Then I’ll have some. I’d like to stay for tea.  
_Nobody in the valley can remember Nova staying for tea. Down by the hedge, Fern drops a whole tray of seedlings._

#### Variant `s24-stowaway-knows`

#### Variant `s24-bun-moss`

**Nova**: I’ll tell him when I get back.  

#### Variant `s24-eternal-watch`

#### Variant `s24-bird-collector`

_Above the falls the ridge goes up into cloud, and the cloud is full of birdsong you don’t know._  
_You stop to eat, and set Fern’s bag of lantern buns on a rock. Then it is a bag of lantern buns minus one._  
_Something enormous is standing in the mist three strides off, with a bun in its beak._  
_It is taller than Nova and striped like a humbug, with legs like stilts and a little crest that won’t lie flat._  
**Tilly** _(shock)_: Oh. Oh. Oh, he’s BEAUTIFUL.  
**Tilly**: He’s called Sir Reginald. That’s decided. Nobody else gets a go.  
_Sir Reginald swallows the bun whole. You can watch it go all the way down._  
_It looks at the bag, and then at you. It takes one step forward and two steps back, the way Sable used to._  
**Nova** _(soft)_: Don’t grab. Whatever you do. A thing like that, you only see once if you grab.  

**Choices**

- **Hold out a bun on your flat hand.** — sets `{"s24-bird":"fed"}`
    - You hold out a bun on your flat hand. The bird looks at your hand for a long time, and takes one step back.
    - Nova holds out a bun of her own without thinking. The bird walks straight past you and takes it from her.
    - **Tilly**: It doesn’t like you. That’s weird. Everything likes Keepers.
    - You think of your pens at home, full of fast grey runners that never make a sound. You don’t say anything.
- **Sit down on the path and wait for it to come to you.** — sets `{"s24-bird":"waited"}`
    - You sit down on the wet path. The bird stays where it is, three strides off, and comes no nearer.
    - Nova sits down too, a little way off. After a long while the bird goes and stands over her instead.
    - **Tilly**: It doesn’t like you. That’s weird. Everything likes Keepers.
    - You think of your pens at home, full of fast grey runners that never make a sound. You don’t say anything.

#### Variant `s24-nest-waited`

_Under an overhang there is a nest the size of a cart wheel, made of heather and string and most of somebody’s washing line._  
_In it are four striped chicks like knitted tea cosies. Beside it sits Tilly, perfectly safe and extremely cross._  
_Her badge sash is wound round the smallest chick, badges and all, so it doesn’t get cold._  
**Tilly** _(angry)_: You took AGES. I’ve been whistling for about a hundred years.  
**Tilly**: Sir Reginald’s a mother. That’s allowed. She can still be Sir.  
_Fairweather’s cage stands open and empty at the edge of the overhang. Out in the dark, a creaky two-note call._  
_You remember the wet path. You sit down by the nest with your Nubbin beside you, and you wait._  
_After a long while the mother comes out of the dark on her own, and stands over her chicks, and over your Nubbin._  
_She tidies the tuft on its head again. It wasn’t bothering it, and she does it anyway._  
**Nova** _(soft)_: There. She came straight home.

_You lift Hazel’s lantern as high as it will go. Nova puts her hand under your elbow, to help hold it up._

#### Variant `s24-tick-scarf`

_Nova sits on the lip of the Shelf with the atlas open at the front, and a stub of pencil worn almost to nothing._

#### Variant `s24-home-eternal`

_You come down off the ridge in the afternoon, slowly, because Augusta Fairweather is coming too, on Nova’s arm._  
**Augusta Fairweather** _(think)_: Fifty years. I don’t suppose anybody has kept my room.  
_She takes the empty cottage at the foot of the falls path, for a week, she says. Nobody believes her, kindly._  
_Your Eternal is on the cottage step when you get in, as if it had been there all along. It probably had._  
_That night you light Hazel’s lantern by the cottage table and open the atlas, to read her list again._  
_In the margins there is writing, in faint pencil. It wasn’t there before, or it was, and it needed this light._  
_It is on every page. Beside a boy and a dragon in a hollow: “I’d have fed the dragon.” Beside a building of endless counters: “Ha. Typical.”_  
_She read every story Nova ever brought her, and had something to say about all of them. Of course she did._  
_Nova stays three days, and has tea every afternoon. On the fourth she goes over the far ridge with a pack you could lift with a finger._  
_At the top she turns and waves, and doesn’t wait to see whether you wave back. She knows._  

#### Variant `s24-home-corvin`

_Corvin is by the pond when you come in, though it isn’t Sunday. He looks at the lantern, lit in broad daylight._  
**Corvin Vale** _(soft)_: Good.  
_He hangs it back on its hook himself, and doesn’t trim the wick. It doesn’t need it._  

#### Variant `s24-lecture`

_The museum is full, which it never is. Somebody has put out every chair, and some of the Nubbins have brought their own._  
**Professor Delve** _(happy)_: A lecture. Every plaque in this museum must be true. This one has taken fifty years to become so.  
**Augusta Fairweather** _(think)_: I was called a liar. I have been wrong about a great many things. I was not wrong about the bird.  
**Augusta Fairweather** _(happy)_: I shan’t tell you where she nests. That is the whole of the lecture. Thank you.  
_Professor Delve hangs the new plaque himself, on tiptoe, with his spectacles on the end of his nose._  
**Professor Delve**: “A tall striped bird nests on the far ridge. Augusta Fairweather saw it first. She was right.”  
**Professor Delve** _(soft)_: True. Entirely true. I checked.  
_Everyone claps. Fairweather sits down rather suddenly, and doesn’t say anything for a while._  

#### Variant `s25-sill-late`

_The lamp comes on: a warm ring of light, and a small, satisfied tick every second, like something counting sheep._  
_Everyone goes down the ladder talking about the moth. You go last, with the toy and the book, and set them on the window seat for now._  
_For now turns into a week, and then a fortnight. The toy sits on the sill and gets the afternoon sun._  
_Nobody looks at it. You don’t either, though you mean to._  
_Out of the window, on top of the western hill, Moss is looking this way. He would tell you he was looking at the weather._  

#### Variant `s25-storm-nosable`

_Later that week your Nubbin finds the toy on the sill. It sniffs it, sneezes, and goes off after a moth._  
_Not yet, then._  
_Then, at the end of a hot week, a storm. It comes over the hill at bedtime all at once, like a dropped tray._  
_Your Nubbin won’t settle. It goes from the basket to the hearth to the door and back, ears flat at every bang._  
_You look for the old sock it likes, and can’t find it. You warm a stone at the hearth, and it pushes the stone away._  
_Thunder, right overhead. The window seat lights up white, and the knitted toy on its sill with it._  

#### Variant `s25-page-found`

_There is a page about the clockwork toys in the nursery, and an old horse who has watched them come and go. You read it to him._  
**You**: “…and he knew that they were only toys, and would never turn into anything else.”  
_Clank listens with his chimney-light steady. His whistle doesn’t drop at all._  
**Moss**: The horse was wrong about that. I’m older than the horse.  
_You pass it on. Clank does a small spin on his one wheel, the way he does when he knew something already._  
**Clank** _(happy)_: Announcement: I found my word already. In Nova’s book. Next to a wooden boy.  
**Clank** _(think)_: Is Real the same word? I don’t think it is the same word.  
**Moss**: No. It lives next door to it. He’s already knocked.  
_You pass that on. Clank’s whistle rises, just a little, and holds._  
**Clank** _(soft)_: Next door. Announcement: I have a neighbour.  

#### Variant `s25-byte`

_A wet afternoon. Moss is on the window ledge again; he says the hill is too damp for his knees._  
_You were not aware he had knees. You decide not to raise it._  
_Byte comes up the path under an umbrella, with his clipboard in a plastic bag, to return a pencil Hazel lent him nine years ago._  
**Byte** _(think)_: She said bring it back when I’d finished with it. I’ve finished with it. It took longer than projected.  
_He sees the toy on the sill, and stops._  
**Byte**: Fern says your Nubbin made her apologise to that. Formally. With the good cups.  
**Byte**: And that you said it was Real. I’d like to know what you meant. Ideally in a unit.  
**Byte**: Hazel had a book for everything. Is there a book?  

#### Variant `s25-page-byte`

_You find the page where the old horse explains it, and read it out. Byte writes it all down._  
_Then he reads back what he’s written, and frowns at it._  
**Byte** _(think)_: There isn’t a single number on this page. Not one. How is anybody meant to check it?  
**Byte**: Can it be measured? Ask him. I know you can hear him. I’ve stopped pretending I think you can’t.  
**Moss**: Tell him to try.  
_You tell him. Byte looks at the toy for a long time, then at his clipboard, then turns to a clean page._  
**Byte**: Fine. A log. Hours held. Times carried. Times dropped, and gone back for in the dark. I’ll let you know.  

#### Variant `s25-shed-kept`

_You carry the bundle out to the shed without him. He is in the tin bath, under the cold water, waiting for your hands._  
_A whole slow evening: cold water, a sliver of soap, and squeezing, and never wringing. Clover checks, and says “again”._  
_Your hands go numb, and then they go patient._  

#### Variant `s25-line`

_It is past dark when Clover says that will do. Your fingers are white and wrinkled, and you can’t feel them._  
_You roll him in a towel and press, and never twist. Clover nods, which from Clover is a medal._  
**Clover**: Now you leave him. Overnight, flat, by the range. Not too near. Wool doesn’t like being rushed any more than the rest of us.  
_His loose eye came off in the rinse. Clover holds out her palm for it._  
**Clover**: I’ll sew it back tomorrow, with proper thread. It’ll outlast both of us.  
_Your Nubbin wakes on the step, looks round for him, and finds him on the towel by the range._  
_It lies down beside the towel: not on it, beside it._  

#### Variant `s25-line-dry`

_In the morning you peg him on the line by his ears. By noon he’s dry: a size smaller, lumpier, and somehow more himself._  
_Clover sews his eye back on, tight, and gives you a spare button from her tin, the same brown, for your Nubbin to wear._  
_He goes back on your Nubbin’s bed, and stays there for good._  
_At teatime Moss comes down to the ledge and looks through the window at him on the bed for a long while._  
**Moss** _(soft)_: Then it’s Real to you. That’s how it ends for most of them. It’s the better ending, mostly.  

#### Variant `s25-falls-kept`

_You come home from the Hollow with the notebook inside your coat. It’s late, and the cottage smells of cold ashes._  
_Moss is on the window ledge. He doesn’t ask about the notebook, and you don’t tell him yet._  
_On your Nubbin’s bed the toy is waiting, a size smaller, with both eyes on tight._  
_There is one thing you do want to tell him._  
_You went along the ledge behind the falls today. You put your hand on the rock, and the rock was warm._  

#### Variant `s25-twice-kept`

**You**: There’s a pool behind the falls. Warm. Steaming a bit. Lit from underneath, by nothing.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: The Wellspring. Yes.  
**Moss** _(think)_: The first ones came up out of the warm water as small lights. Nobody ever told me where the lights came from.  
_He looks through the window at your Nubbin, asleep on its bed with its arms round the toy._  
**Moss** _(soft)_: I’ve a guess. It’s only a guess.  
**Moss**: He can stay where he is while I think about it.  

#### Variant `s25-coda-wild-button`

_Spring. The flowers on the hill are open, and the long grass at the edge of the Grove is new and green and full of wild ones._  
_One of them is sitting right at the edge, where the mown grass stops. You haven’t seen it before._  
_Its coat is grey-brown and soft and faintly lumpy, like something knitted by somebody who dropped a stitch every night._  
_The tuft on top of its head is a little crooked. One of its eyes catches the light like a button._  
**You** _(soft)_: Button?  
_It turns its head._  
_It doesn’t come in, and it doesn’t run either. It looks at you the way you look at someone you’re nearly sure you know._  
_Then it hops, twice, high and neat, as if to show you that it can._  
_It goes back into the long grass. In the mornings, after that, it is often at the edge._

#### Variant `s25-coda-coat-button`

_Spring. Fern and Clover come up the lane together with a parcel, which is suspicious, because they never agree about wrapping._  
**Fern** _(happy)_: We couldn’t decide whose idea it was. So it was both of ours, and any mistakes are Clover’s.  
**Clover**: There aren’t any mistakes. There are three, and they’re on purpose.  
_Inside is a knitted coat, Nubbin-sized, in grey-brown wool, with a crooked bobble on the hood. It has one brown button._

**Fern**: Inside the collar. Look.  
_Stitched inside the collar, in Fern’s wobbly red cross-stitch: BUTTON._  
_Your Nubbin puts it on, and goes and sits on its bed beside the toy, so that everybody can see they match._  
**Clover** _(soft)_: Matching. Oh, I could cry. I am crying. Don’t look.

_Moss is watching your Nubbin drag the toy through the daisies by one ear._

#### Variant `s25-moss-gone`

_Moss is looking at the long grass again._  
**Moss** _(soft)_: Nothing. I’m only looking. I’m allowed to look at grass. It is most of what I do.  

#### Variant `s25-clank-next-door`

**Clank** _(happy)_: Announcement: I live next door to a word. Moss says so.  
**Clank** _(soft)_: Next door is a very good place to live. You can wave.  

#### Variant `s26-door-thrums`

_Then the wind comes along the wall, and lifts the ivy like a curtain._  
_Under it, exactly at the height of Linnet’s eyes, is a round iron knob._  
**Linnet** _(happy)_: There. There! You lot stand too tall to see anything.  
_The hum in the old wall is loudest here. Every little round door in the mortar has a face in it, watching._  
**The Thrums**: Good morning, Keeper. Yes. That is a door. We have lived beside it for ever.  
**The Thrums** _(think)_: Nobody asked us about it. We did not like to mention it. We are now checking whether we should have.  
**Linnet** _(shock)_: Is the wall talking?  
**You**: Mostly it’s agreeing with itself.  
_The key goes in as if it has been waiting. It takes both your hands to turn it, and then Linnet’s as well._  

#### Variant `s26-ramp-1`

_First evening of the ramp. Dusty takes the top step out in one go, and stands back, pleased._  
**Linnet** _(think)_: That’s not a ramp. That’s a hole with ambitions.  
**Dusty**: Give it time.  
_Linnet sits at the top, beside Sparks’s coil of wire, and tells you where every barrow of earth goes. She is right about all of it._  

#### Variant `s26-ramp-2`

_Second evening. It is nearly a ramp now, and Dusty wants to call it done._  
**Linnet** _(angry)_: It’s too steep. I’d go down that in one second flat, straight into the rose.  
**Dusty**: It’s fine. I walked down it.  
**Linnet**: You walked down it. Dig it longer.  
_Dusty digs it longer. It takes the rest of the evening, and afterwards Dusty admits that Linnet was right._  

#### Variant `s26-ramp-3`

_Third evening, and the last barrow goes in. The ramp runs from the door to the path, long and shallow and tamped hard._  
_Linnet goes down it before anyone can offer anything._  
_At the bottom she doesn’t say a word. She goes straight to the wick rose and puts her hand on it._  
_Dusty leans on the spade and looks at nothing in particular, for quite a long time._  

#### Variant `s26-lift-1`

_Next evening Sparks lifts Linnet down again: front and back, never the sides, exactly as instructed._  
**Sparks** _(happy)_: Smooth! Like a generator. A small, rude generator.  
**Linnet**: Thank you.  
_She says it every time. It seems to cost her a little more every time._  

#### Variant `s26-lift-2`

_Second evening. Sparks is late: a fuse at the pond lantern._  
_Linnet waits at the top of the steps for half an hour, watching you and Dusty work below._  
**Linnet** _(think)_: I don’t mind.  
_She minds. She has pulled every ivy leaf within reach off the wall, and laid them out in order of size._  
**Sparks** _(shock)_: Sorry! Sorry! Right. Front and back.  

#### Variant `s26-lift-3`

_Third evening. Sparks lifts her down, and then has to go, and says she’ll be back to lift her up again._  
**Linnet** _(think)_: What if you’re not?  
**Sparks** _(happy)_: Then I’ll be back later!  
_Linnet doesn’t answer that. She goes to the wick rose and stays there, as far from the steps as the garden allows._  

#### Variant `s26-tobin-bench-cellar`

**Old Tobin** _(happy)_: This puts me in mind of a story. Hazel’s grandchild here came down the mill cellar with me. Five levels.  
**Old Tobin** _(happy)_: Six. Eels as long as your arm. Rats in hats. A wheel the size of the moon. Go on, tell her.  
**You**: Four levels. The eels were annoyed. Nobody had a hat.  
**Old Tobin** _(think)_: That’s the dull version. Where was I.  
_He stops. He looks at the roses, and at Linnet, up to her elbows in a raised bed, and not tipping._  
**Old Tobin** _(soft)_: I kept a door shut once, for twenty years. I thought I was sparing people. It got heavy.  
_He sits on, and stops arguing, and watches her work until it’s dark._  
_On the way out, at the door, he puts his hand flat on the old wall._  
**Old Tobin** _(soft)_: My old gaffer used to say: put a rose where your worry sits, and the worry has to go and sit somewhere else.  
**Old Tobin**: He was a terrible gardener. He was right about that, though.  

#### Variant `s26-snowdrops-lifted`

_Her tracks go along the old wall to the open door, and stop._  
_Linnet is at the top of the three steps. They are under a foot of snow, and nobody has cleared them, because nobody thought anyone would come._  
**Linnet** _(angry)_: An HOUR. I’ve been here an hour. I can see them from here. Look at them.  
_Down by Hush’s bench, through the snow, there are snowdrops. She has been looking at them for an hour._  
**Linnet** _(angry)_: Sparks is at the falls. Dusty’s up the cliff. Grandad can’t. Everyone who lifts is somewhere else.  
**Linnet** _(angry)_: It isn’t me that’s stuck. It’s these stupid steps.  
**You**: Then let’s get rid of them.  
**Linnet** _(shock)_: Now? In this?  
**You**: Now. You tell me where.  
_So you dig, in the snow, with frozen hands and Dusty’s spare spade, while Linnet directs from the top like an admiral._  
**Linnet** _(think)_: Shallower. Shallower! I’ll go over backwards on that. Make it longer, not steeper. LONGER.  
_It takes all morning, and it isn’t pretty. When it’s done she goes down it before you can offer, and doesn’t look back._  
_Snowdrops. All round Hush’s bench, white heads bowed, as if they came up to listen._  
_Some are in Linnet’s bed, in a dead straight row. The rest are everywhere: under the bench, in the path, one right in the middle of the ramp._  
**Linnet** _(happy)_: Your Nubbin’s. It can’t count and it can’t plant, and they all came up anyway.  
**Linnet** _(soft)_: I’d have got in on my own. If anybody had built it so I could.  

#### Variant `s26-complete-post`

_Winter sun comes over the old wall and lies along the beds. The garden is still mostly bare, but it is not grey._  
_Dusty is hanging the ivy on a rail over the door, so it swings like a curtain. The robin is supervising._  
_The key hangs on the old wall post, under the lantern. By the end of the week, three people you have never met have been in to look._  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: The stone from the old steps is still in a heap. I’m making you a bench out of it, like Hush’s. For the Grove.  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: And this. I wore it every day Fossil and I went digging. It’s yours now. You dig like someone who means it.  
_It is a gardener’s apron, earth worked into every seam. Linnet crowns you with snowdrops, and says you can’t take them off until tea._  
_Linnet’s mum sits on Hush’s bench with Tobin, and they argue about which of them she gets her chin from._  
**Linnet** _(happy)_: Me. I get it from me.  
_On the way out you look back: the bench, the beds, the white pebble by the bare patch, and the snowdrops in the path._  
_The robin has decided it lives with you now, in a general sort of way. The door stays open behind you._

#### Variant `c12-wall-garden`

_At the old wall, Hazel stops._  
_The door in the wall stands open. She has walked past this wall for forty years, and never once seen a door in it._  
_She goes in, slowly, one hand on the stone, and down the ramp without thinking about it, the way you do with a good one._  
_Bare roses, pruned. Snowdrops round the bench, and a few in odd places, where somebody lost count._  
_The robin comes down onto the back of Hush’s bench, and looks at her as if she owes it something._  
_She stands among the roses for a long while, and says nothing. It is a different nothing from the pond._  
_Then she picks one rosehip off a cane, and puts it in her buttonhole._  
_On the way out she touches the old wall post, and keeps her hand there a little longer than at the others._  

#### Variant `end-moss-sleeps-garden`

_His flower came up on the hill, by the flat stone where he always sat._  
_Linnet says that by midsummer its seed will be over the old wall, the way he went once a year._  
_Into the bit of earth by Hush’s bench, the one he chose, where her white pebble still is._  
_She says the robin will see to it. Nobody argues, and nobody makes a speech._  

#### Variant `end-epi-garden`

_The door in the old wall isn’t locked any more. Linnet comes every summer and tells everyone where to dig._  
_Hush’s roses come out in June, all at once, as if somebody told them to. Somebody did._  

#### Variant `end-epi-garden-cold`

_The door in the old wall stands open, and none of yours go in. They sit outside it in the sun, quite still._  
_Linnet came the first summer. She looked at them for a long time, and asked what was wrong with them._  
_You said they were safe. She went home a week early, and didn’t come again._  

#### Variant `s27-row-sold`

_Through the shed window, past the spider and her word, you can see the runners’ pens._  
_One pen to each runner, in a straight row, every rail white with frost. Every runner grey, and none of them moving._  
_At the very end of the row is one facing the wrong way. It is looking down the valley, towards the Grove._  
_You know the shape of it. You knew it before you knew you were looking._  
_It was yours, and you sold it in the spring for two hundred Shells. There is a number painted on its side: 9._  
_Outside, a lad with two buckets goes along the pens, whistling. He stops whistling at the end of the row._  

#### Variant `s27-words-sold`

_Fern is up a ladder in her orchard, losing an argument with a plum tree._  
**Fern**: A word? For a spider? Oh, I’ve got one. Somebody’s. As in, this one is somebody’s.  
**Fern** _(sad)_: When Bramble went, people said “it’s only a Nubbin.” She wasn’t only. She was mine. Somebody’s.  
_Clover listens with her head on one side, the way she listens to the shy ones._  
**Clover** _(soft)_: Warm. You can’t make a grey one not grey. But you can make it warm. Warm is allowed.  
_Luna is in her night garden at noon, asleep in a deckchair. She answers without opening her eyes._  
**Luna** _(soft)_: Home. Everything in my garden grows towards it in the dark. Even the moonflowers. Especially them.  
_Byte takes the question very seriously, and goes away, and comes back with a sheet of paper._  
**Byte** _(think)_: “Statistically above average.” It’s accurate. It’s kind. It’s the nicest thing I know how to say about anybody.  
_You read the four words to the shed window. Spindle listens to each, legs folded, exactly like a judge._  
_At Byte’s she goes very still for a long moment. Then she points one leg, delicately, at the length of the rail._  
**Sparks** _(happy)_: She’s saying it won’t fit. I’d tell him gently. He’s sensitive about his adjectives.  
_Spindle spins three short threads and pulls them tight, the way someone squares a pile of letters on a desk._  
_Then she points at you, and then at the end of the row, and waits._  
**Sparks** _(think)_: She wants to know what it’s called. Its real name. Not the paint.  
_You tell her the name you gave it the day it hatched. It is strange to say it out loud up here._  
_Spindle writes it once, small, in the corner of the window, to practise. She gets it right first time._  

#### Variant `s27-midnight-shield`

_At midnight a lamp comes on in the upstairs window of the house, and stays on._  
_Corvin is sitting at the window. He is watching you: Hazel’s heir, with a lantern, at his rail, in the middle of the night._  
_He watches you all night. He watches your hands, and the lantern, and the way you walk the rail a step at a time._  
_He never once looks at the rail itself: he thinks he knows who is writing on it. He is wrong, and you let him be._  
_Spindle works on below the lantern, a thread at a time, and Corvin never sees her. Up here, she is your secret._  
_Near dawn the upstairs lamp goes out, and Spindle ties off the last thread. You can’t feel your fingers._  

#### Variant `s27-home`

_By breakfast half the valley is at the Circuit fence. Nobody arranged it; people just came._  
_Along the whole finishing straight, in letters as tall as a Nubbin, the frost says: HOME._  
**Fern** _(happy)_: HOME. Look at the size of it. She must have run out of rail.  
_Mr Tull is at the gate with his cart. He reads the rail, and then the faces along the fence._  
**Mr Tull** _(think)_: Morning, Mr Vale. I’ve come for whatever didn’t place. But I’ll not take that one.  
**Mr Tull**: Its name’s been up on your rails all summer. I’d have half this valley at my gate by Sunday, asking after it.  
**Mr Tull**: I sell runners. I don’t sell the ones people ask after. Good day to you.  
_He tips his hat to the fence, quite politely, and drives away over the pass. His cart is lighter by one._  
_Corvin stands at the end of the straight with his hands in his pockets. He reads the rail one more time._  
_Hob is at the pen behind the house. He opens his mouth to say “Nine”, and stops, and says its name instead._  
_Its real name, the one you gave it. He says it carefully, like a word in another language._  
**Corvin Vale** _(think)_: It was yours, I believe. Before.  
**Corvin Vale**: Two hundred Shells. What I gave you for it. Not a Shell more. I’ll not have it said I made a profit off Hazel’s heir.  

**Choices**

- **Count out two hundred Shells.** — sets `{"boughtBack":true,"s27-paid":"shells"}`

- **Let the Keepers pass a hat.** — sets `{"boughtBack":true,"s27-paid":"hat"}`

    - Sparks borrows Byte’s and goes along the fence with it, and it comes back heavy. Fern puts in a button.
    - **Fern**: It’s a good button. It’s off my good coat.
    - Corvin counts the hat out on the rail, in silence. Two hundred Shells exactly, and a button.
    - **Corvin Vale**: …There’s a button.
    - **Fern**: It’s a very good button.
    - He looks at it. Then he puts it in his coat pocket: not the breast pocket, the other one.

#### Variant `s27-gate-sold`

_At the Grove gate, before you can lift and push, your old Nubbin noses at the bottom bar, the way it used to._  
_It remembers the gate sticks. It remembers._  
_It sits down by the gatepost and looks at the Grove for a long time, as if checking it is all still there._  
_The 9 on its side will wash off by the spring. You don’t hurry it._  

#### Variant `s27-carry-clank`

_Clank has followed you up the valley, because somebody said the word “ladder” in his hearing._  
**Clank** _(happy)_: Announcement! I cannot climb! I have one wheel! But I am VERY good at steady!  
_Sparks carries her long ladder in from the yard, sideways, swearing gently at the door frame._  
**Sparks**: Rafters are your job. I’ve got the knees of an eighty-year-old and the opinions to match.  
_Your Nubbin looks at the ladder, and at the sac, and at you. Then it climbs, one rung at a time, with enormous care._  
_At the bottom, Clank holds the ladder with both arms and does not move one single bolt._  
**Clank** _(soft)_: Steady. Steady. Announcement, but quietly: steady.  
_It comes down backwards with the sac held against its chest, the way you’d carry an egg that might still hatch today._  
_Spindle watches every rung, and doesn’t breathe out until it is on the floor. You don’t either._  
**Sparks**: Grove lantern shed. Top shelf, over the warm pipe. Nothing up there ever freezes, not even my tea.  
_You carry it home down the valley in your two cupped hands. Spindle stays in her window; she has done her part._  

Sets `{"s27-carrier":"nubbin"}`

#### Variant `s27-carry-wisp`

_Something grey drops from the shed beam beside the sac, so quietly that Sparks yelps._  
_Wisp. He has trailed you up the valley, three hedges behind, the way he follows anything warm._  
_He has his torn ear against the sac. It has been in the sun on that beam all afternoon._  
**Wisp** _(soft)_: warm. sun-warm.  
**Wisp** _(soft)_: mine. warm. mine.  
**You**: Not yours. Hers. But you could keep it warm for her. Under your step, all winter. Till spring.  
_Wisp thinks about this with his whole body. It is the kind of bargain he understands: you get to hold the warm thing._  
**Wisp** _(soft)_: …keep it warm. warm is keeping. yes.  
_He takes it down the wall in his mouth, the way you would carry the last warm thing in the world._  
_Spindle watches every step he takes, then lifts two legs at him, politely. Wisp hisses, and is pleased._  

Sets `{"s27-carrier":"wisp"}`

#### Variant `s27-spring`

_The first spring. The snow has gone from the lane, and the wind comes up the valley smelling of wet earth._  
_On the warm shelf in the lantern shed, the little straw-coloured sac has opened._  
_Out come spiders, hundreds of them. Each is smaller than a crumb, and perfectly sure of itself._  
_They climb the shed wall, and the gatepost, and the top bar of the gate that sticks. They line up along it._  
_Then, one by one, each lets out a thread into the wind, and lifts, and floats away over the valley._  
_You stand at the gate and watch them go until they are just glints. It takes all morning, and you don’t mind._  
_Three stay._  
_The next morning, in the corner of the lantern shed window, there is a web no bigger than a thumbnail. It says EVENING._  
_You laugh out loud, on your own, in the shed. Then you say it back: “Evening.”_  
_It is not often that someone comes along who is a true friend and a good writer._  
_Spindle was both._  

#### Variant `s27-spring-wisp`

_The first spring. The snow has gone from the lane, and the wind comes up the valley smelling of wet earth._  
_Under the cottage step, where Wisp has hoarded it all winter like treasure, the little straw-coloured sac has opened._  
_The first thing the spiders see is a grey head with one torn ear, very close, watching them with enormous care._  
_Wisp lets them climb over his nose, and does not hiss once. Then they climb the gate, and line up along the top bar._  
_One by one, each lets out a thread into the wind, and lifts, and floats away over the valley._  
_Wisp watches them go until they are just glints. Then he goes back under the step and sulks, magnificently._  
_Three stay._  
_The next morning, in the corner of the lantern shed window, there is a web no bigger than a thumbnail. It says EVENING._  
_You laugh out loud, on your own, in the shed. Then you say it back: “Evening.”_  
_It is not often that someone comes along who is a true friend and a good writer._  
_Spindle was both._  

#### Variant `s28-null-minder`

_Byte’s front room. The Minder ticks on the shelf: the clock face, the three little bells, the chute._  
_Null sits in the little bed under it, perfectly upright, waiting. Its blue has gone thin at the edges._  
**Byte** _(think)_: This is Null. It’s on a schedule. Don’t mind the clock. It’s an experiment. It’s going very well.  
_Parcel bows to Null, but Null doesn’t look up. It is watching the bell._  
_Parcel considers this. Then it climbs into the bed beside Null, folds its paws, sits very upright, and waits for the bell too._  
_Byte goes pale, then paler. You didn’t know he had another shade in him._  
**Byte** _(shock)_: No. Not that one. That one isn’t…  
**Byte** _(sad)_: It isn’t mine. It’s on loan. You don’t put a thing that isn’t yours on a clock.  
_He lifts Parcel out, gently, and carries it into the kitchen. Then he comes back and stands looking at the lever on the side of the Minder._  
_He doesn’t pull it, not yet. But he looks at it for a long time._

#### Variant `s28-bench-bramble`

#### Variant `s29-kitchen-after`

_The door opens a crack. An old woman with a candle glares out: a quilted dressing gown, and a soot-black apron over it._  
**Granny Brock** _(angry)_: Who’s ringing my bell at this hour? Nobody rings it at any hour. That’s why it’s there.  
**Marina**: Granny Brock. It’s Marina, from the river. We’re lost, and this one’s frozen.  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: Marina. Hm. And a frozen one. And somebody’s grandchild, by the chin on it. And a… what is that, in your coat?  
_Your Nubbin looks out at her, and she looks back. Neither of them blinks first, and something is settled._  
**Granny Brock**: In. All of you. Boots off. Not you, you’ve not got boots. In.  
_The passage runs back into the bank and opens into a kitchen older than the Wood: stone flags, a red fire, hams on hooks._  
_The walls are carved with bits of words nobody can read now. It is the ruin of a house older than anyone’s grandmother._  
**Granny Brock**: Sit. Eat. Then talk, if you must. Better if you mustn’t.  
_She feeds you soup, and bread, and more soup. She puts Dusty nearest the fire and drops a blanket on them without looking._  
**Granny Brock** _(think)_: The Wood? It looks. That’s all it does. It looks at folk who don’t belong to it, and they run, and that’s their lookout.  
**Granny Brock**: My people burned charcoal in this wood before your grandmothers were born. There was a town here once. Now there’s me.  
**Granny Brock**: People come and go. The Wood stays. So do I.  
_Dusty listens to every word, very hard, the way you listen to someone old when someone old at home has only just gone._  
_When the fire gets low, Granny Brock puts another log on without being asked. Nobody goes to sleep for a long time._  

#### Variant `s29-low-river-full`

_Summer’s end. Since the rain on the hill, Dusty has been sleeping in Marina’s boathouse, and nobody has said a word about it._

**Marina** _(happy)_: Look at that. Sitting down. I’ve made a sailor of you.  
_The river is full to its banks, and running fast and clear the way it ought to at the end of a summer._  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Look at her go. Since a certain gate got opened, she’s been running like she means it.  
_You row clean up through the shallows without touching bottom once. Dusty keeps time under their breath: in, pull, out._  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: I’m doing it. I’m actually doing it. Don’t look at me, I’ll stop.  
**Marina** _(happy)_: Not looking. Eyes on the weir. Not looking at all.

#### Variant `s29-night-ribbon`

#### Variant `s29-ford-ribbon`

_The sun is up when you come round the bend to the ford. The old otter is still on his stone, just as you left him, and so is Dusty._

_Marina is wiping her face with her sleeve, again and again. It doesn’t seem to be working._  
**Marina** _(soft)_: Don’t know what’s the matter with me. Must’ve been the early start.

**Marina** _(happy)_: That’s him saying he’s done with it. Same thing, with otters.

**Marina** _(think)_: On the island. Asleep in the grass. That’s all.

#### Variant `s30-wall-s1`

_The frosts have come, and the Grove is white at dawn round the fourth post. Moss, after his long talk, has not gone back up the hill._  
_You find him at the old wall, by Hush’s lantern post, with frost on his back that he hasn’t bothered to shake off._  
**Moss** _(think)_: Clover’s gold one. Luna told me. Luna tells me things at dawn, when I am too tired to argue.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: You know she sat with the still ones. You walked here with me, and heard her ask to be kept.  
**Moss**: I never told you what she thought sad was for.  
**Moss** _(soft)_: People thought it made her a sad thing, the sitting. They were wrong about which part was sad.  
**Moss**: Low in the wall. The flat stone, by your foot. Look at it properly. She meant it to be looked at.  

#### Variant `s30-hole-youngest`

_Then your youngest Nubbin comes down the bank: the one Fossil sat with until dark. It climbs into the hole and sits on Dusty’s boot._  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: Oh, not you as well.  
_Dusty laughs, wetly, gets mud in their mouth, and laughs at that too._  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: She trained you both. Didn’t she. The old madam. Sat on a sill for twenty years and trained the lot of you.  

#### Variant `s30-gone-helped`

_Early autumn. Clover is at your gate before it is light, in her dressing gown and wellies._  
**Clover** _(shock)_: She’s gone. Dimple. Her basket was cold when I got up.  
**Clover** _(sad)_: She’s taken nothing but the cushion off the sill. Fossil’s cushion, toys and all.  
**Clover**: What does she want with it? She’s done nothing but pile rubbish on it for weeks.  
**Clover** _(sad)_: Drizzle’s under the rain butt and won’t budge. The others are all in a heap. I didn’t know who else to come to.  
_Dusty is coming up the lane with a spade on one shoulder, early as always. One look at Clover, and they put the spade down._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: She’s going back to where the happy was. I would. I’ve been trying to all summer.  
_The three of you set off down the lane in the half dark._  

#### Variant `s30-trail-s5`

_On the bank of your pond, where you held Fossil up in the shallows, there is a sock, laid out neatly._  
_Under Hazel’s crooked tree, half a wooden peg, just where the wrinkled fruit once fell into Fossil’s lap._  
**Dusty** _(shock)_: She’s doing the walk. Fossil’s walk. Backwards. The water, the tree, the cliff.  
**Dusty** _(sad)_: I told it at Clover’s table every night for a fortnight. I thought she was asleep under the sill.  
_From the tree the trail runs east along the stream, a pine cone, then a cotton reel, towards the striped cliff._  
_At the foot of the cliff the rock is striped like a cake. Where it meets the ground, there is a hollow, dug down and in._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: That’s it. The spoon hole. Where I dug her out. Two days, with a spoon.  
_Dimple is down in it, on Fossil’s cushion. She is scraping at the back of the hollow with both hands, as if there might be another egg._  
_The hollow is only waist-deep on you, but on a Nubbin the lip is a cliff. She can’t climb out, and she hasn’t tried._  
_Dimple says nothing. She scrapes, and scrapes, and smiles at the rock._  

#### Variant `s30-hollow-helped`

_Drizzle is at home under the rain butt. So it is you who climbs down, carefully, and sits in the dust at the back of the hollow._  
_Dimple turns, and the smile goes on like a lamp. She bounces at you, and offers you a pebble, a root, a handful of dust._  
_She performs. She runs the streamer race on her own in a space the size of a bath, and does the hat toss without a hat._  
_You don’t clap, and you don’t stop her. Your knees go cold, and then numb._  
**Clover** _(shock)_: Shall I come down? I should come down. I’m her— I should—  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: Leave them a minute. Look at her. She’s nearly out of tricks.  
_It takes a long time. It takes longer than the party did._  

#### Variant `s30-hollow-youngest`

_Your youngest Nubbin goes over the lip after them, sliding down on its bottom, and sits on Dimple’s other side._  
_Fossil sat with it once, until dark. It seems to remember how._  
**Dusty** _(soft)_: Course it does.  

#### Variant `s30-hill-helped`

_That afternoon Dimple walks up the hill. Not on anyone’s shoulder: up the path, past the halfway place, slowly, on her own feet._  
_You walk beside her, and every few steps she checks that you are still there. Dusty walks behind with Fossil’s cushion, and doesn’t hurry anybody._  
_At the top is Fossil’s flower, sandstone-coloured, a little bent by the frost. Dimple sits down in front of it._  
_You sit on one side of her. Dusty sits on the other, cross-legged, and sets the cushion in the grass, dent up._  
_Nobody does anything. The wind goes over, and down in the valley somebody is burning leaves._  
_Your Nubbin, who has copied Dimple all autumn, comes and sits down too. It copies the sitting perfectly._  

#### Variant `s30-complete-helped`

_On the way home you stop at the old wall. Clover holds the paper flat; you rub over Hush’s stone with a stub of charcoal from her range._  
_The marks come up black and fine under your hand: rings, and lines, and rings with lines through them._  
**Clover** _(soft)_: Dimple ought to have a mark of her own. On her page. A proper one.  
_In the clean corner of the paper, you draw it for her: a ring with a line through it._  
_Clover takes the charcoal off you and draws one more beside it, small and close: a plain ring, just touching. Yours._  
**Clover** _(soft)_: That’s the both one. That’s her now.  
_That night you write it up properly. Hush’s stone, and a page for each of your own Nubbins, ringed and lined from what you remember._  
_In the morning there is a parcel on your step: a pale-blue rain hat, Nubbin-sized, and Fossil’s cushion, plumped for the first time in years._  
_The note says: “Drizzle’s spare hat; she has three. And the cushion: Fossil would want it sat on, not kept. —C”_  
_Under it, in loud capitals: “ALSO A RIBBON, BLUE AND GOLD. I DYED IT TWICE TO GET IT RIGHT. —SPARKS”_  

#### Variant `s30-after-clover`

_Clover’s shed door stands half shut every dusk now. Fidget sees to it herself; she has decided half is the right amount._  
_On the deep kitchen sill, Dimple and Drizzle sleep in the sun, gold and pale blue, one pressed against the other._  
**Clover** _(soft)_: She still smiles most of the day. I wouldn’t change it. And now when she isn’t, I know where to look.  

#### Variant `s30-after-dusty`

**Dusty** _(soft)_: I go up and sit with Fossil on Thursdays. Dimple comes. Gets there first, some weeks. Waits for me.  
**Dusty** _(happy)_: We don’t do anything. It’s the best bit of the week. Don’t tell Clover. She’ll bring a flask.  

#### Variant `s30-after-byte`

**Byte** _(think)_: I’m keeping the chart. For science. Dimple’s ball, every time I see her.  
**Byte** _(soft)_: Mostly gold. Sometimes blue. Sometimes both, which my scale didn’t cover, so I drew a new column. It’s my favourite column.  
**Byte** _(happy)_: Also she pulled my laces on Tuesday. Both of them. I’ve logged it as a result.  

#### Variant `s30-after-sparks`

**Sparks** _(happy)_: I sat still! On the hill! With Dimple! For…  
**Sparks** _(happy)_: Fifty-one seconds. Byte timed it. Nearly a minute. That’s a personal best. That’s a valley record.  
**Sparks** _(soft)_: It was really nice. It was horrible. It was really nice.  

#### Variant `s31-guising-sat`

_Late in the evening a ghost comes back up the lane on its own, dragging its bag. It stops at the edge of the long grass._  
**Ned** _(soft)_: Hello. I came to see if he’d come yet.  
**Tam**: Not yet. He’s busy being sincere somewhere.  
**Nettie** _(think)_: What did you get? Did you get toffee? I didn’t want any. I’m only asking.  
_Ned turns his bag upside down. Five rocks come out, one after another, with a sound like somebody walking on a beach._  
_One of them has a little paper label tied on with string._  

#### Variant `s31-guising-party`

_Halfway through the party, the guisers come in from the lane on a gust of cold air: a witch, three bats, and a ghost made mostly of holes._  
_The bats and the witch tip their bags out on Sparks’s workbench. Toffee apples, nuts, pennies, a whole honey cake._  
_Ned tips his out last. Five rocks come out, one after another, and roll in among the spanners._  
**Sparks** _(sad)_: Oh, Ned.  
_Pixel stops playing the pianola in the middle of a tune, out of respect._  
_One of the rocks has a little paper label tied on with string._  

#### Variant `s31-if-away`

_Nearly midnight. Three lanterns burn steady across the Grove, and the fourth post stands dark over the long grass._  
_Two small shapes sit beside it under a patchwork quilt and a borrowed blanket. The spare blanket stays folded on the sign._  
_Tam says the spare is for the Great Gourd, in case he’s cold from flying._  
_Along the far hedge Luna’s lantern moves slowly, as it does every night. Tonight it passes the long grass more often than usual._  
**Nettie** _(sad)_: Tam. My feet have gone.  
**Nettie**: Tam. What if he isn’t coming?  
**Tam**: He’s coming. And when he comes, I’ll put in a good word for everybody. You. Ivy, even. The Keeper, even. Even though they went.  
**Tam** _(happy)_: If he comes, I’ll ask him to—  
_He stops. He heard it too._  
**Tam** _(sad)_: …If.  
**Tam** _(sad)_: I said “if”. Out loud. In the patch. He’ll have heard it. Hearing things is the whole point of him.  
_He pulls the quilt over his head. Nettie pats it, the way you pat a dog that has been told off._  

#### Variant `s31-midnight-wisp`

_Just after midnight, something thin and grey comes out of the dark under the hedge. One of its ears is torn to a fringe._  
_It goes straight to the sign, takes Tam’s toffee apple off the top, hanky and all, and carries it a little way off._  
_Then it sits down in full view and eats it, very loudly, with its back to them._  
_Tam grips Nettie’s arm._  
**Tam** _(soft)_: Is that him?  
**Nettie**: That’s a thief.  
_Wisp licks the hanky clean, thanks nobody, and goes back under the hedge._  

#### Variant `s31-shape-sable`

_A grey shape rises out of the white grass by the post. It comes slowly, and the frost doesn’t seem to touch it at all._  
_Tam takes one look, says “Oh,” very politely, and faints clean away._  
_It is Sable. The frost can’t do anything more to her than it has already done._  
_She looks at Tam, flat out in his quilt. Then she looks at you, for a long time._  
_Then she sits down beside you, close enough to touch, and faces the dark lane with you._  
_Sable says nothing. Her ears stay up._  
_You don’t say anything either._  

#### Variant `s31-shape-away`

_A shape rises at the edge of the white grass, where the frost hasn’t reached. Round, and goggled, and trailing a scarf._  
_Tam takes one look, says “Oh,” very politely, and faints clean away._  
_You come back down the lane with a lantern before it’s light. Tam is flat out in his quilt, and your Nubbin is asleep on top of him._  
**Nettie** _(angry)_: Your Nubbin came out of the grass in its glasses, and he FAINTED, and I’ve been the only one awake for HOURS.  
**Nettie** _(angry)_: I didn’t get any toffee. I didn’t get ANYTHING. I sat in a field.  

#### Variant `s31-four-sable`

_At four o’clock exactly, a lantern comes bobbing down the lane, grumbling._  
**Ivy**: Four o’clock. I said four. Look at the state of—  
_She sees Sable. Ivy has an opinion on everything, and she hasn’t got one about this._  
_She looks at Tam, fainted in his quilt, and doesn’t laugh. She hands her lantern to Nettie and lifts him, quilt and all._  
_He doesn’t wake. His boots hang down, and she hitches him up and doesn’t complain once._  
_She has a blanket over her arm, brought for him. He doesn’t need it; he has the quilt._  
_On the way past, she drops it on you without looking._  
**Ivy**: You look frozen. It’s very stupid, sitting out all night. I’m not saying anything nice about it.  
_For Ivy, that is something nice._  
_Sable doesn’t move. She lets Ivy pass within an arm’s length, and only her ears turn._  
_Ivy goes off up the lane with Tam, and Nettie trots after with the lantern, yawning, very small._  

#### Variant `s31-four-away`

_At four o’clock exactly, a lantern comes bobbing down the lane, grumbling._  
**Ivy** _(angry)_: Four o’clock. I said four. Look at the state of him.  
_She looks at Tam, fainted in his quilt, and doesn’t laugh. She hands her lantern to Nettie and lifts him, quilt and all._  
_He doesn’t wake. His boots hang down, and she hitches him up and doesn’t complain once._  
_She doesn’t look at you. There is a blanket over her arm, and she keeps it._  
**Ivy**: Nettie. Home.  
_Nettie stamps off up the lane after them with the lantern, furious all the way, and very small._  

#### Variant `s31-light-sable`

_Up the lane, a light goes on in the end cottage. Ivy gets Tam up the stairs, takes his boots off, and tucks him in._  
_Then she walks back to her own room and shuts the door. Nobody ever hears about it from her._  
_Sable stays beside you until it is light. Neither of you moves._  
_When the sun reaches the top of the hill, she gets up, shakes the frost off her back, and goes to see about breakfast._  
_Tam’s sign is still standing, WELCOME GREAT GOURD, with the R still backwards for luck._  

#### Variant `s31-promise-went`

_When Ned has gone home, Tam turns round on the wall and looks at you, and then looks away._  
**Tam**: I don’t need anybody, you know. To wait. I can do it on my own. I nearly did.  
**Tam**: Nettie doesn’t count. She was asleep for the important bit.  
_He gets down off the wall and goes home, holding his quilt up off the mud._  
_Next morning there is a second sign in the long grass, smaller, beside the first. It is painted on the lid of a biscuit tin._  
_It says: SEAT FOR A KEEPER (IF)._  

#### Variant `s31-complete-strays`

_Next morning, Tam is down at the long grass before anyone else is up. By the foot of his sign, someone has left something._  
_A small heap of fruit: windfall plums, two apples, and a pear with one bite out of it. Someone has left it for the strays._  
**Tam** _(happy)_: He got the letter. He couldn’t carry it all the way to the Hollow, so he left it here for us to take.  
_You put it out at the edge of the Grove that evening. By morning it has all gone, and nobody saw who ate it._  
_Sparks won’t take the goggles back. She says they’ve seen action now, and it would be wrong._  
_Your gourd lantern goes on the wall by the gate, where it can be seen from the air. Its face is still mostly thinking about smiling._  

#### Variant `s31-complete-busy`

_Next morning, Tam is down at the long grass before anyone else is up. By the foot of his sign, someone has left something._  
_…Or hasn’t. There is nothing at all: just the sign, the frost going off it, and the long grass._  
**Tam** _(think)_: Nothing. He didn’t come and he didn’t send anything. Because we said he needn’t, if he was busy.  
**Tam** _(happy)_: So he was busy. And he was polite back. That’s proof, that is.  
_Sparks won’t take the goggles back. She says they’ve seen action now, and it would be wrong._  
_Your gourd lantern goes on the wall by the gate, where it can be seen from the air. Its face is still mostly thinking about smiling._  

#### Variant `s31-ambient-tam`

**Tam** _(think)_: I’m practising sitting. For next year. You have to start early or your knees go.  

#### Variant `s31-ambient-ivy`

**Ivy**: I’ve told Tam the Great Gourd isn’t real eleven times. He writes it down each time. I think he’s keeping score.  

#### Variant `s31-ambient-ned`

**Ned** _(happy)_: I gave my best rock away. Byte says I can start again. He’s bringing me a granite.  

#### Variant `s31-ambient-nettie`

**Nettie**: I’m going guising next year. I’ve decided. Unless Tam says.  

#### Variant `s31-ambient-lantern`

**Tam** _(happy)_: Did you see? The fourth one lit. By itself. At dawn. Nobody touched it.  
**Tam** _(happy)_: It was him. The Great Gourd. I told you that patch was sincere.  
**Tam**: Ivy says he’s very late. I say he’s very thorough.
